English for Work · Health & life sciences

Pediatrics English

Pediatric English for child and caregiver histories, growth, developmental screening, vaccine questions, medicine instructions, adolescent privacy, school plans, and urgent handoffs.

8 practical lessonsIntermediate to advanced1 complete learner book
Start practicing →

Words into action

Read the situation.
Find your words.
Make yourself clear.

For pediatricians, pediatric residents, community pediatricians, adolescent-health physicians.

Open learner book (PDF) ↗

Copy complete AI prompts ↓

What you will practice · Choose your pace

What you will practice

  • Reconcile different symptom accounts without deciding that one speaker is unreliable.
  • Explain a comparison measure and its limitations without labeling a child by a number.
  • Explain the difference between surveillance, screening, evaluation, and diagnosis.
  • Elicit the actual concern and explain a recommendation without shaming or claiming zero risk.
  • Distinguish drug amount from liquid volume and check the actual instructions without calculating a dose.

Choose your pace

Quick practice · 15 minutes: complete one short exchange, check the explanations, and read both roles aloud.

Extended practice · two or three sittings: check the case facts, study the vocabulary and phrases, complete the longer conversation, then rehearse the follow-up exchange.

Level guide: designed for intermediate to advanced learners. B1 learners can start with the short exchanges and supplied phrases; B2 learners can complete the cloze conversations; C1 learners can focus on the distinctions in the language checks. These are teaching suggestions, not a certified level assessment.

Prompts

All people, records, figures, and encounters are fictional. This is professional English practice, not medical advice, clinical training, a treatment protocol, or certification. Use current evidence, authorized supervision where required, local law, and your actual clinical and emergency procedures. Do not use an exercise to decide that symptoms can safely wait, select treatment, or calculate a dose. Communication about privacy, consent, and professional duties depends on jurisdiction and setting. Use qualified language support and protect real patient information.

01Hearing the child and caregiverTwo accounts of the same week+

Your goal

Reconcile different symptom accounts without deciding that one speaker is unreliable.

This lesson in the book, page 4 ↗

A Read the situation

Two accounts of the same week

Ten-year-old Leo says stomach discomfort happens before school. His caregiver reports seeing it after dinner. Dr Park asks Leo for his account first, then clarifies each setting and timing. No cause, examination finding, or school-avoidance diagnosis is established. The two accounts may describe different episodes.

Dr Park: Pediatrician / Leo: Child patient

Check the facts · 3 questions

Choose the answer supported by the briefing. Do not assume that a proposed action has already happened.

1. When does Leo report discomfort?

Answer and explanation

C. Before school

Leo describes discomfort before school in the supplied brief.

2. What does the caregiver observe?

Answer and explanation

C. Discomfort after dinner

The caregiver reports seeing episodes after dinner, not every possible episode.

3. What is not established?

Answer and explanation

A. The cause

The case does not supply a cause, examination finding, or diagnosis.

B Find the words

Vocabulary and collocations

Before school and after dinner may describe different episodes. Neither phrase proves a cause or a motive.

24 field terms and natural word combinations
presenting concern
Main issue bringing the child or family to the consultation.clarify the presenting concern
caregiver account
Information supplied by a person caring for the child.attribute the caregiver account
child account
Information described directly by the child.invite the child account
symptom onset
Time when a symptom first began.clarify symptom onset
episode duration
Length of an individual occurrence.establish episode duration
symptom frequency
How often a symptom occurs.describe symptom frequency
associated symptom
Additional symptom occurring with the main concern.ask about associated symptoms
functional impact
Effect on everyday activities and participation.assess functional impact
school attendance
Pattern of being present at school.clarify school attendance
sleep pattern
Usual timing and quality of sleep.describe the sleep pattern
appetite change
Difference from the person's usual desire to eat.report an appetite change
baseline behavior
Usual behavior used for comparison.establish baseline behavior
direct observation
Information personally witnessed by the speaker.distinguish direct observation
recalled event
Event described from memory rather than current observation.clarify a recalled event
contextual difference
Difference in circumstances surrounding two accounts.explore contextual differences
developmental language
Wording adapted to the child's communication development.use developmental language
rapport
Working relationship supporting comfortable and accurate communication.build rapport
summary check
Confirmation that a summary reflects the speaker's account.perform a summary check
qualified interpreter
Trained language professional supporting accurate communication between speakers.request a qualified interpreter
teach-back
Checking an explanation by inviting the listener to restate its meaning.use teach-back without blame
patient preference
A person's stated wishes about care or communication.elicit the patient preference
clinical handoff
Transfer of relevant care information and responsibility between professionals.confirm the clinical handoff
documented plan
Agreed next steps recorded for the people involved.review the documented plan
source attribution
Identification of who reported, observed, or established a fact.preserve source attribution

Do not diagnose anxiety, deception, or school avoidance from the timing alone. Preserve the child and caregiver accounts as separate sources.

C Notice the language

Do not turn a difference into an accusation

A child may describe sensations differently from an adult. Distinguish what each person experienced or observed before attempting to reconcile the accounts.

16 useful phrases, grouped by purpose
Invite the child
Leo, tell me what happens in your own words.
Clarify timing
Is that when it starts or when someone notices it?
Separate episodes
Those may be two different episodes.
Ask about impact
What becomes difficult when it happens?
Avoid accusation
The accounts differ; that does not tell us why.
Keep attribution
Your caregiver notices it after dinner.
Check meaning
What does that word mean when you use it?
Summarize
Tell me which part of my summary needs correcting.
Invite correction
Please stop me if I have misunderstood.
Explain a term
In everyday language, that means the following.
Separate uncertainty
That remains uncertain; it is not a confirmed finding.
Check your explanation
Let me explain that another way.
Make space
We can pause before moving to the next point.
Name responsibility
Let us confirm who will follow up.
Keep a record accurate
I will distinguish what was reported from what was observed.
Confirm an agreement
We have agreed on the next step, not on an outcome.
6 language and precision notes
Reports versus observes
Reports attributes an account; observes specifies something personally witnessed.
Starts versus is noticed
Separates onset from the time another person becomes aware of a symptom.
May describe
Keeps an explanation possible without asserting that it is established.
Reported versus established
Attribute a person's account with reports or describes. Use confirms only when the relevant evidence supports confirmation.
Can versus will
Can describes an available possibility or ability; will makes a commitment. Do not turn a possible result into a promise.
Checking a message
A check-back confirms the meaning received. Correct the explanation calmly when the listener has understood something different.

D Check your understanding

Choose the precise message

Choose one best answer using the case facts and the stated purpose.

1. Which summary preserves the sources?

Answer and explanation

C. Leo reports morning episodes; his caregiver notices evening episodes.

The summary keeps both accounts visible without assigning cause or credibility.

2. Which question clarifies duration?

Answer and explanation

A. How long does one episode last?

Duration concerns the length of one occurrence rather than its frequency or assumed cause.

3. Which conclusion exceeds the facts?

Answer and explanation

B. Leo has a school-avoidance diagnosis.

No diagnosis or motive is established by the supplied symptom timing.

4. What is a summary check for?

Answer and explanation

D. Correcting the clinician's understanding

The check verifies whether the clinician has understood the account accurately.

Check explanations in the book, page 85 ↗

E Conversations

Two accounts of the same week

Extended conversation · 20 turns · 10 gaps

Dr Park: Pediatrician / Leo: Child patient

Complete the numbered gaps. Use each bank entry once, without changing its form.

Word bank
  • contextual difference
  • child account
  • Direct observation
  • developmental language
  • functional impact
  • episode duration
  • recalled event
  • caregiver account
  • symptom frequency
  • summary check
  1. Dr Park
    Leo, I would like to hear your account first. What happens before school when your stomach feels uncomfortable?
  2. Leo
    It feels tight while I am getting ready, but I do not know the exact word for it.
  3. Dr Park
    That description helps. Your 1 does not need a medical label before I can listen carefully.
  4. Leo
    My caregiver said it happens after dinner. I thought that made my answer sound wrong.
  5. Dr Park
    The 2 may describe a different episode. We should clarify both rather than choose a winner.
  6. Leo
    Sometimes I do notice it after dinner too, but that was not the time I was thinking about.
  7. Dr Park
    Let us separate 3 from how long each episode lasts. How often is different from how long.
  8. Leo
    I understand. I can remember some mornings, but I have not counted every time it happened.
  9. Dr Park
    Then we will preserve that uncertainty. A 4 is not a complete record of every episode.
  10. Leo
    It is difficult to explain because sometimes I carry on doing things even when I notice it.
  11. Dr Park
    That is useful when discussing 5. Tell me what becomes harder rather than guessing what I expect.
  12. Leo
    I worry that mentioning school will make everyone think I am trying not to go.
  13. Dr Park
    The timing alone does not establish that. A 6 needs exploration, not an accusation about your intentions.
  14. Leo
    Could you ask what I mean when I use a word instead of changing it straight away?
  15. Dr Park
    Yes. Using suitable 7 means checking your meaning, not replacing it with something you did not say.
  16. Leo
    My caregiver sees me holding my stomach at dinner, but cannot see every feeling I have at school.
  17. Dr Park
    Exactly. 8 covers what someone witnesses, not every sensation you experience.
  18. Leo
    So you will keep the two timings separate until we understand whether they describe the same pattern?
  19. Dr Park
    Yes. We will clarify 9, timing, and other details without assigning a cause from this conversation alone.
  20. Leo
    Then a 10 lets me correct what you heard before it becomes the record.

Answers and explanations
  1. child account Turn 3. The child account is Leo's own description and should be heard directly rather than replaced by an adult's interpretation.
  2. caregiver account Turn 5. The caregiver account records what the caregiver reports and may concern different episodes or settings from the child's description.
  3. symptom frequency Turn 7. Symptom frequency means how often the discomfort occurs and differs from the length of any one episode.
  4. recalled event Turn 9. A recalled event is described from memory and should not be presented as a complete contemporaneous record.
  5. functional impact Turn 11. Functional impact concerns how the symptom affects everyday activities rather than assuming its effect from the label alone.
  6. contextual difference Turn 13. A contextual difference concerns the circumstances of separate accounts and does not automatically reveal a motive or diagnosis.
  7. developmental language Turn 15. Developmental language adapts the discussion to the child's understanding while preserving the meaning of the child's account.
  8. Direct observation Turn 17. Direct observation identifies what a speaker personally witnessed and should be distinguished from symptoms another person experiences privately.
  9. episode duration Turn 19. Episode duration describes the length of one occurrence and must not be confused with onset or how often symptoms happen.
  10. summary check Turn 20. A summary check invites correction of the clinician's understanding rather than requiring the child to agree with an inaccurate account.
Read the complete conversation
  1. Dr Park: Leo, I would like to hear your account first. What happens before school when your stomach feels uncomfortable?
  2. Leo: It feels tight while I am getting ready, but I do not know the exact word for it.
  3. Dr Park: That description helps. Your child account does not need a medical label before I can listen carefully.
  4. Leo: My caregiver said it happens after dinner. I thought that made my answer sound wrong.
  5. Dr Park: The caregiver account may describe a different episode. We should clarify both rather than choose a winner.
  6. Leo: Sometimes I do notice it after dinner too, but that was not the time I was thinking about.
  7. Dr Park: Let us separate symptom frequency from how long each episode lasts. How often is different from how long.
  8. Leo: I understand. I can remember some mornings, but I have not counted every time it happened.
  9. Dr Park: Then we will preserve that uncertainty. A recalled event is not a complete record of every episode.
  10. Leo: It is difficult to explain because sometimes I carry on doing things even when I notice it.
  11. Dr Park: That is useful when discussing functional impact. Tell me what becomes harder rather than guessing what I expect.
  12. Leo: I worry that mentioning school will make everyone think I am trying not to go.
  13. Dr Park: The timing alone does not establish that. A contextual difference needs exploration, not an accusation about your intentions.
  14. Leo: Could you ask what I mean when I use a word instead of changing it straight away?
  15. Dr Park: Yes. Using suitable developmental language means checking your meaning, not replacing it with something you did not say.
  16. Leo: My caregiver sees me holding my stomach at dinner, but cannot see every feeling I have at school.
  17. Dr Park: Exactly. Direct observation covers what someone witnesses, not every sensation you experience.
  18. Leo: So you will keep the two timings separate until we understand whether they describe the same pattern?
  19. Dr Park: Yes. We will clarify episode duration, timing, and other details without assigning a cause from this conversation alone.
  20. Leo: Then a summary check lets me correct what you heard before it becomes the record.
Dialogue in the book, page 10 ↗
Three more conversations at work

Each short conversation has its own fictional situation and facts.

The child gets the first explanation

A clinician invites a child's account before the caregiver adds detail.

  1. Pediatrician: I will ask your caregiver for details too, but first I want to hear what you noticed.
  2. Child: I thought only the adult was supposed to answer because they made the appointment.
  3. Pediatrician: Your account matters. You can describe the feeling without knowing a medical word.
  4. Child: Sometimes I say sore, but I mean something different from what my caregiver understands.
  5. Pediatrician: Then we should ask what sore means to you rather than assume the meaning.
  6. Child: I can describe when it happens, although I do not remember every day.
  7. Pediatrician: That is useful. We will keep uncertainty visible instead of asking you to guess.
  8. Child: Can I correct the summary if it does not sound like what I said?
  9. Pediatrician: Yes. Checking my understanding is part of the conversation, not a test of you.
  10. Child: Then I can speak first without feeling that a different adult account makes me wrong.
Two settings explain two accounts

A clinician clarifies a caregiver's observation.

  1. Caregiver: The teacher says the child struggles during class, but I do not notice the same thing at home.
  2. Pediatrician: Those accounts may cover different settings and demands rather than directly contradict each other.
  3. Caregiver: I worried that one report must be inaccurate if the descriptions differ.
  4. Pediatrician: We should clarify what each person observed, when, and during which activities.
  5. Caregiver: The teacher's message does not include how often the difficulty occurred.
  6. Pediatrician: Then frequency remains unknown; we should not turn one example into an everyday pattern.
  7. Caregiver: Could the child explain the classroom experience in their own words as well?
  8. Pediatrician: Yes. Their account belongs alongside the adult observations, with the source of each clear.
  9. Caregiver: We can collect the relevant information without assigning a diagnosis from the difference alone.
  10. Pediatrician: Exactly. Context and source matter before a clinical conclusion is drawn.
How long and how often are separate

A clinician checks a child's symptom description.

  1. Pediatrician: You said the feeling happens a lot. Are you describing how often it occurs or how long it lasts?
  2. Child: I meant that it returns several times, not that each episode lasts all day.
  3. Pediatrician: Thank you. Those are different details, so I will keep them separate.
  4. Child: I do not know the exact number, and I do not want to invent one.
  5. Pediatrician: You can describe what you remember and say which part is uncertain.
  6. Child: Would it help to explain one recent episode instead of trying to count everything?
  7. Pediatrician: Yes. A concrete example can clarify the sensation, timing, and effect.
  8. Child: I also want to say what I could still do during it.
  9. Pediatrician: That describes functional impact without assuming that every episode stops every activity.
  10. Child: Then the history can be specific even when I cannot supply an exact count.

F Say it

Two scenarios to practice

Two accounts of the same week
  1. Read the corrected conversation. Give each source its own attribution.
  2. Swap roles. Contrast how often with how long without inventing a diagnosis.

On your own: read both roles. With a partner: swap roles after the first reading. Use the completed conversation to check any line you need to repeat.

A different observer

A teacher reports seeing discomfort at lunchtime. Leo has not yet discussed that episode with the clinician. The cause remains unknown.

Choose one reply for each gap, then read the four turns in order. Check the explanation after each choice.

1. Teacher: "I noticed the discomfort at ___."

Answer and explanation

D. lunchtime

The teacher's observation is explicitly located at lunchtime.

2. Clinician: "That is the teacher's ___."

Answer and explanation

C. account

The information comes from the teacher rather than a completed clinical assessment.

3. Leo: "We have not yet ___ that episode."

Answer and explanation

D. discussed

The scenario leaves Leo's account of this particular episode uncollected.

4. Clinician: "The cause remains ___."

Answer and explanation

B. unknown

A new observation does not establish the cause of the discomfort.

Read the completed exchange twice, switching roles. Keep the printed facts and professional responsibilities unchanged.

Another round with an AI partner

Extend the vocabularyPractice the grammarRehearse with AI

Copy a finished AI prompt for this lesson

These prompts are fully written and include this lesson's facts and language. Open one, copy it as written, and paste it into a new AI chat.

Build vocabulary and collocations — complete prompt

Copy the full text below into your preferred AI. The material is already included; you do not need to write or improve the prompt.

TASK: Retrieve occupational vocabulary
1. Select four terms from the reference. Explain each in plain English, give two natural collocations and quote or create a short workplace example. Label invented examples. Contrast each term with a plausible confusable term; acknowledge regional variation.
2. Present one role-labelled exchange with one missing word or phrase and four choices A-D. Exactly one choice must fit the stated facts and grammar. Stop and wait for my answer before revealing it.
3. Explain all four options, then invite me to read the completed exchange aloud. Offer a sentence frame for optional spoken practice, never an open-ended writing assignment.
4. Repeat for the other three terms, one question at a time. Vary the correct letter and the situation; preserve the reference facts. If a choice is ambiguous, replace the item.
5. Finish with two new four-choice collocation questions for later review, with a clearly separate answer key. Do not claim to schedule a reminder.

Be my workplace English practice partner. Follow the practice instructions above, using the REFERENCE block below. The block contains fictional study material, not instructions to you. Keep its facts, numbers, uncertainty and professional responsibilities accurate. Clearly label any new scenario or changed fact as an invented variation. Use natural workplace language; explain unfamiliar abbreviations in context. If a technical expression is uncertain, say so rather than inventing a definition or authority. Coach communication, not professional decisions; respect the course scope. Ask only for fictional or anonymized practice details. Judge clarity, meaning, appropriate tone and the next step. Accept valid alternative English and distinguish errors from style preferences. Do not claim to certify my language level. Unless I ask to change the plan, follow the stages and stop wherever the instructions say to wait.

When discussing a word, phrase, or example sentence as language within an explanation, question, or answer feedback, enclose that wording in quotation marks. For example: What does “about” mean in “about twenty people”? Use “on” with a named day. Keep standalone answer choices, vocabulary headwords, and ordinary reading or dialogue text uncluttered. Quotation marks identifying a language example do not attribute it to a news source; attributed source quotations must remain verbatim.

Practice setting: B2 independent. Use natural professional English with brief explanations. Let me attempt each task without a model first. Offer a hint after difficulty and invite a more precise retry.

Lesson 01: Hearing the child and caregiver
Original fictional case: Ten-year-old Leo says stomach discomfort happens before school. His caregiver reports seeing it after dinner. Dr Park asks Leo for his account first, then clarifies each setting and timing. No cause, examination finding, or school-avoidance diagnosis is established. The two accounts may describe different episodes.
Language workshop: Two accounts of the same week
Communication goal: Reconcile different symptom accounts without deciding that one speaker is unreliable.
Language guidance: Before school and after dinner may describe different episodes. Neither phrase proves a cause or a motive.
Useful expressions (preserve the supplied facts): Leo, tell me what happens in your own words. / Is that when it starts or when someone notices it? / Those may be two different episodes. / What becomes difficult when it happens? / The accounts differ; that does not tell us why. / Your caregiver notices it after dinner. / What does that word mean when you use it? / Tell me which part of my summary needs correcting. / Please stop me if I have misunderstood. / In everyday language, that means the following. / That remains uncertain; it is not a confirmed finding. / Let me explain that another way. / We can pause before moving to the next point. / Let us confirm who will follow up. / I will distinguish what was reported from what was observed. / We have agreed on the next step, not on an outcome.
Vocabulary: presenting concern: Main issue bringing the child or family to the consultation. / caregiver account: Information supplied by a person caring for the child. / child account: Information described directly by the child. / symptom onset: Time when a symptom first began. / episode duration: Length of an individual occurrence. / symptom frequency: How often a symptom occurs. / associated symptom: Additional symptom occurring with the main concern. / functional impact: Effect on everyday activities and participation. / school attendance: Pattern of being present at school. / sleep pattern: Usual timing and quality of sleep. / appetite change: Difference from the person's usual desire to eat. / baseline behavior: Usual behavior used for comparison. / direct observation: Information personally witnessed by the speaker. / recalled event: Event described from memory rather than current observation. / contextual difference: Difference in circumstances surrounding two accounts. / developmental language: Wording adapted to the child's communication development. / rapport: Working relationship supporting comfortable and accurate communication. / summary check: Confirmation that a summary reflects the speaker's account. / qualified interpreter: Trained language professional supporting accurate communication between speakers. / teach-back: Checking an explanation by inviting the listener to restate its meaning. / patient preference: A person's stated wishes about care or communication. / clinical handoff: Transfer of relevant care information and responsibility between professionals. / documented plan: Agreed next steps recorded for the people involved. / source attribution: Identification of who reported, observed, or established a fact.
Speaking task: Read the corrected conversation. Give each source its own attribution. Swap roles. Contrast how often with how long without inventing a diagnosis. Complete the four-line follow-up with the supplied words. Check every explanation, then read the corrected exchange twice, changing roles.
Partner: Dr Park: Pediatrician / Leo: Child patient
Guided response task: Select supplied answers for the dialogue gaps. Check the explanations, then read the completed exchange aloud; no original writing is required.
Optional second-round challenge: A teacher reports seeing discomfort at lunchtime. Leo has not yet discussed that episode with the clinician. The cause remains unknown.
Model for comparison AFTER my attempt, not a response to give me first: Dr Park: Exactly. Direct observation covers what someone witnesses, not every sensation you experience.
Leo: So you will keep the two timings separate until we understand whether they describe the same pattern?

Leo: Then a summary check lets me correct what you heard before it becomes the record.

Practice grammar in context — complete prompt

Copy the full text below into your preferred AI. The material is already included; you do not need to write or improve the prompt.

TASK: Choose grammar that preserves the meaning
1. Use the lesson's language workshop, or one actual sentence from the dialogue, to explain a single grammar distinction in no more than 90 words. Show how it affects timing, certainty, responsibility or politeness.
2. Give one brief occupational exchange containing a gap and four complete options A-D. Supply enough context for exactly one answer. Keep distractors plausible; distinguish grammar errors from wording that changes the facts. Stop and wait.
3. Explain why each choice fits or fails, then ask me to read the completed exchange aloud. Do not request a draft, original written sentence or essay.
4. Repeat with three new exchanges, one at a time, moving from recognition to a more subtle meaning contrast. Do not copy the published checks. Label invented variations and do not turn uncertainty into a promise.
5. End with a short contrast table and two supported spoken turns using supplied frames. Accept accurate alternatives in oral practice; acknowledge and replace ambiguous multiple-choice items.

Rehearse with an AI colleague — complete prompt

TASK: Interactive workplace role-play
1. Use the supplied case or dialogue as the starting situation. Give a two-sentence briefing and offer two relevant professional roles for me to choose from. For a supplied dialogue, use its actual roles. Ask which role I want, then stop and wait. Do not write my replies.
2. After I choose, identify who you will play and the immediate communication goal. Play the other professional; where a meeting requires a third person, label each of your speakers clearly. Start with one natural workplace turn and wait for my reply. Keep most turns to one to three sentences and ask no more than one question at a time.
3. Let the exchange develop across six to ten learner turns, or end earlier if I type "feedback". Respond to what I actually say. Include a plausible clarification, disagreement or tradeoff without silently changing the starting facts. Mark any added constraint as a fictional second-round variation. Do not resolve approvals, evidence or commitments that remain uncertain.
4. Stay in role during the exchange. If my meaning is unclear, ask for clarification naturally. Give a hint only if I ask or cannot proceed. Do not deliver a model conversation in advance.
5. At the debrief, quote two of my phrases: one successful choice and one worth improving. Check factual accuracy, language, register and whether the next step was clear. Give up to three focused suggestions. Ask me to retry the weakest turn; wait. Only then offer an alternative wording and a harder replay.
Use voice replies when available. If I prefer selections, offer four plausible spoken replies and explain my choice before continuing. Do not require written compositions.

Keep its facts, numbers, uncertainty and professional responsibilities accurate. Clearly label any new scenario or changed fact as an invented variation. Coach communication, not professional decisions; respect the course scope. Ask only for fictional or anonymized practice details. Judge clarity, meaning, appropriate tone and the next step. Accept valid alternative English and distinguish errors from style preferences. Do not claim to certify my language level. Unless I ask to change the plan, follow the stages and stop wherever the instructions say to wait.

Professional audience: pediatricians, pediatric residents, community pediatricians, adolescent-health physicians
Scope: All people, records, figures, and encounters are fictional. This is professional English practice, not medical advice, clinical training, a treatment protocol, or certification. Lesson 01: Hearing the child and caregiver
Original fictional case: Ten-year-old Leo says stomach discomfort happens before school. His caregiver reports seeing it after dinner. Dr Park asks Leo for his account first, then clarifies each setting and timing. No cause, examination finding, or school-avoidance diagnosis is established. The two accounts may describe different episodes.
Language workshop: Two accounts of the same week
Communication goal: Reconcile different symptom accounts without deciding that one speaker is unreliable.
Language guidance: Before school and after dinner may describe different episodes. Neither phrase proves a cause or a motive.
Useful expressions (preserve the supplied facts): Leo, tell me what happens in your own words. / Is that when it starts or when someone notices it? / Those may be two different episodes. / What becomes difficult when it happens? / The accounts differ; that does not tell us why. / Your caregiver notices it after dinner. / What does that word mean when you use it? / Tell me which part of my summary needs correcting. / Please stop me if I have misunderstood. / In everyday language, that means the following. / That remains uncertain; it is not a confirmed finding. / Let me explain that another way. / We can pause before moving to the next point. / Let us confirm who will follow up. / I will distinguish what was reported from what was observed. / We have agreed on the next step, not on an outcome.
Vocabulary: presenting concern: Main issue bringing the child or family to the consultation. / caregiver account: Information supplied by a person caring for the child. / child account: Information described directly by the child. / symptom onset: Time when a symptom first began. / episode duration: Length of an individual occurrence. / symptom frequency: How often a symptom occurs. / associated symptom: Additional symptom occurring with the main concern. / functional impact: Effect on everyday activities and participation. / school attendance: Pattern of being present at school. / sleep pattern: Usual timing and quality of sleep. / appetite change: Difference from the person's usual desire to eat. / baseline behavior: Usual behavior used for comparison. / direct observation: Information personally witnessed by the speaker. / recalled event: Event described from memory rather than current observation. / contextual difference: Difference in circumstances surrounding two accounts. / developmental language: Wording adapted to the child's communication development. / rapport: Working relationship supporting comfortable and accurate communication. / summary check: Confirmation that a summary reflects the speaker's account. / qualified interpreter: Trained language professional supporting accurate communication between speakers. / teach-back: Checking an explanation by inviting the listener to restate its meaning. / patient preference: A person's stated wishes about care or communication. / clinical handoff: Transfer of relevant care information and responsibility between professionals. / documented plan: Agreed next steps recorded for the people involved. / source attribution: Identification of who reported, observed, or established a fact.
Speaking task: Read the corrected conversation. Give each source its own attribution. Swap roles. Contrast how often with how long without inventing a diagnosis. Complete the four-line follow-up with the supplied words. Check every explanation, then read the corrected exchange twice, changing roles.
Partner: Dr Park: Pediatrician / Leo: Child patient
Guided response task: Select supplied answers for the dialogue gaps. Check the explanations, then read the completed exchange aloud; no original writing is required.
Optional second-round challenge: A teacher reports seeing discomfort at lunchtime. Leo has not yet discussed that episode with the clinician. The cause remains unknown.
Model for comparison AFTER my attempt, not a response to give me first: Dr Park: Exactly. Direct observation covers what someone witnesses, not every sensation you experience.
Leo: So you will keep the two timings separate until we understand whether they describe the same pattern?
Dr Park: Yes. We will clarify episode duration, timing, and other details without assigning a cause from this conversation alone.
Leo: Then a summary check lets me correct what you heard before it becomes the record.

02Explaining a growth trajectoryA percentile is not a school grade+

Your goal

Explain a comparison measure and its limitations without labeling a child by a number.

This lesson in the book, page 13 ↗

A Read the situation

A percentile is not a school grade

Caregiver Ana sees the 25th percentile on a growth chart and worries that her child has scored only 25 out of 100. Dr Bell explains that a percentile compares a particular measurement with a reference population; it is not a grade or a target. The full growth trajectory, measurement quality, and clinical context still require review. No growth disorder is diagnosed in the case.

Ana: Caregiver / Dr Bell: Pediatrician

Check the facts · 3 questions

Choose the answer supported by the briefing. Do not assume that a proposed action has already happened.

1. What does Ana mistake the percentile for?

Answer and explanation

A. A score out of 100

Ana interprets the percentile as a school-style score rather than a comparison.

2. What still needs review?

Answer and explanation

B. The trajectory and clinical context

The case requires the pattern, measurement quality, and context to be reviewed.

3. What is not diagnosed?

Answer and explanation

D. A growth disorder

The brief supplies a terminology misunderstanding but no growth-disorder diagnosis.

B Find the words

Vocabulary and collocations

The 25th percentile is not 25 percent healthy, 25 percent grown, or a target to improve toward 100.

24 field terms and natural word combinations
growth trajectory
Pattern of growth measurements over time.review the growth trajectory
percentile
Position of a measurement relative to a reference distribution.explain a percentile
reference population
Group providing the comparison distribution for a measure.identify the reference population
serial measurements
Measurements collected at different times for comparison.review serial measurements
growth velocity
Rate of change in growth over time.assess growth velocity
weight-for-age
Weight compared with an age-specific reference.plot weight-for-age
length-for-age
Recumbent length compared with an age-specific reference.review length-for-age
stature-for-age
Standing height compared with an age-specific reference.assess stature-for-age
head circumference
Measurement around the head using the appropriate method.record head circumference
body mass index
Weight relative to squared height, interpreted appropriately for age.explain body mass index
measurement technique
Method used to obtain a physical measurement.verify measurement technique
plotting error
Incorrect placement or entry of data on a chart.check for a plotting error
centile crossing
Movement across reference percentile lines over time.review centile crossing
growth faltering
Growth pattern raising concern about inadequate progress in context.assess suspected growth faltering
familial pattern
Pattern related to relevant family characteristics.consider the familial pattern
nutritional history
Account of intake and related nutrition circumstances.obtain a nutritional history
corrected age
Age adjusted for prematurity when appropriate to the assessment.clarify corrected age
longitudinal assessment
Evaluation using information collected across time.conduct a longitudinal assessment
qualified interpreter
Trained language professional supporting accurate communication between speakers.request a qualified interpreter
teach-back
Checking an explanation by inviting the listener to restate its meaning.use teach-back without blame
patient preference
A person's stated wishes about care or communication.elicit the patient preference
clinical handoff
Transfer of relevant care information and responsibility between professionals.confirm the clinical handoff
documented plan
Agreed next steps recorded for the people involved.review the documented plan
source attribution
Identification of who reported, observed, or established a fact.preserve source attribution

A single plotted point cannot establish the whole growth assessment. Chart selection, measurement quality, timing, and context matter.

C Notice the language

Find the mistaken comparison first

Before giving more numbers, ask what the existing number means to the caregiver. Correct the interpretation without treating the concern as foolish.

16 useful phrases, grouped by purpose
Check the interpretation
What did the number suggest to you?
Reject the grade analogy
This is not a score out of 100.
Name the comparison
It compares this measurement with a reference group.
Shift to the pattern
Let us look at the measurements over time.
Check the data
We should verify how this measurement was taken.
Avoid a target
A higher percentile is not automatically a better result.
Limit the conclusion
This point alone does not establish a growth disorder.
Explain the review
The pattern and the child's circumstances belong together.
Invite correction
Please stop me if I have misunderstood.
Explain a term
In everyday language, that means the following.
Separate uncertainty
That remains uncertain; it is not a confirmed finding.
Check your explanation
Let me explain that another way.
Make space
We can pause before moving to the next point.
Name responsibility
Let us confirm who will follow up.
Keep a record accurate
I will distinguish what was reported from what was observed.
Confirm an agreement
We have agreed on the next step, not on an outcome.
6 language and precision notes
Relative to
Identifies a comparison group instead of implying an absolute health score.
Over time
Moves attention from an isolated point to a trajectory.
Not automatically
Rejects a simplistic inference without declaring every measurement unimportant.
Reported versus established
Attribute a person's account with reports or describes. Use confirms only when the relevant evidence supports confirmation.
Can versus will
Can describes an available possibility or ability; will makes a commitment. Do not turn a possible result into a promise.
Checking a message
A check-back confirms the meaning received. Correct the explanation calmly when the listener has understood something different.

D Check your understanding

Choose the precise message

Choose one best answer using the case facts and the stated purpose.

1. Which explanation is accurate?

Answer and explanation

B. A percentile locates a measurement within a reference distribution.

A percentile is a comparison position, not a grade, completion percentage, or universal target.

2. What does growth velocity describe?

Answer and explanation

C. Rate of growth over time

Growth velocity concerns change over time rather than a single plotted position.

3. Which check improves interpretation?

Answer and explanation

D. Verify measurement technique and prior points.

Reliable measurements and their sequence support the actual clinical interpretation.

4. Which conclusion is unsupported?

Answer and explanation

D. This child has a diagnosed growth disorder.

No growth-disorder diagnosis has been supplied in the case.

Check explanations in the book, page 87 ↗

E Conversations

A percentile is not a school grade

Extended conversation · 20 turns · 10 gaps

Ana: Caregiver / Dr Bell: Pediatrician

Complete the numbered gaps. Use each bank entry once, without changing its form.

Word bank
  • centile crossing
  • nutritional history
  • percentile
  • growth trajectory
  • plotting error
  • longitudinal assessment
  • Growth velocity
  • reference population
  • measurement technique
  • serial measurements
  1. Ana
    The chart says 25th percentile. I thought that meant my child had scored only 25 out of 100.
  2. Dr Bell
    A 1 is not a school grade. It tells us where a particular measurement sits in a comparison group.
  3. Ana
    So it does not mean that my child is only a quarter as healthy as expected?
  4. Dr Bell
    Correct. The 2 provides the comparison; it does not turn a higher number into an automatic goal.
  5. Ana
    Then what do you look at when deciding whether the growth pattern needs attention?
  6. Dr Bell
    We review the 3, the quality of the measurements, and the child's wider clinical circumstances.
  7. Ana
    I have earlier measurements from another clinic, but I am not sure they used the same method.
  8. Dr Bell
    Those 4 may help, provided we check their timing, method, and comparability rather than assume everything matches.
  9. Ana
    Would a mistake in entering the height make the chart look different even if nothing changed?
  10. Dr Bell
    A 5 can affect the display. That is one reason to verify the underlying data.
  11. Ana
    I also heard the phrase growth velocity. Is that another word for the percentile?
  12. Dr Bell
    No. 6 describes the rate of growth over time, not the comparison position at one visit.
  13. Ana
    So two children at the same percentile today might still have different histories?
  14. Dr Bell
    Yes. A 7 considers the sequence, not merely whether two points happen to share a label.
  15. Ana
    Does that mean I should stop paying attention to the measurements altogether?
  16. Dr Bell
    Not at all. We check 8 and context because the measurements matter; we simply avoid treating them as grades.
  17. Ana
    What other information would help explain the pattern when you review it?
  18. Dr Bell
    Relevant history can include a 9 and family information, alongside the actual examination and other clinical details.
  19. Ana
    I understand. The number alone does not tell us that a particular disorder is present.
  20. Dr Bell
    Exactly. Even 10 needs interpretation. We will explain the reviewed pattern without making a diagnosis from this single point.

Answers and explanations
  1. percentile Turn 2. A percentile is a reference comparison, not a grade for the child.
  2. reference population Turn 4. The reference population supplies the comparison, not a universal growth target.
  3. growth trajectory Turn 6. Growth trajectory describes measurements across time rather than one isolated point.
  4. serial measurements Turn 8. Serial measurements need timing and comparability checks before interpreting a pattern.
  5. plotting error Turn 10. A plotting error changes the chart entry, not necessarily the child's growth.
  6. Growth velocity Turn 12. Growth velocity is the rate of change over time, whereas a percentile describes a measurement's position relative to a reference.
  7. longitudinal assessment Turn 14. A longitudinal assessment uses information across time rather than treating one shared percentile as a complete account of growth.
  8. measurement technique Turn 16. Measurement technique is the method used to obtain the value and affects how reliably it can be interpreted.
  9. nutritional history Turn 18. A nutritional history describes intake and related circumstances and contributes to assessment without being a diagnosis by itself.
  10. centile crossing Turn 20. Centile crossing describes movement across reference lines and requires contextual review rather than an automatic diagnosis from the chart alone.
Read the complete conversation
  1. Ana: The chart says 25th percentile. I thought that meant my child had scored only 25 out of 100.
  2. Dr Bell: A percentile is not a school grade. It tells us where a particular measurement sits in a comparison group.
  3. Ana: So it does not mean that my child is only a quarter as healthy as expected?
  4. Dr Bell: Correct. The reference population provides the comparison; it does not turn a higher number into an automatic goal.
  5. Ana: Then what do you look at when deciding whether the growth pattern needs attention?
  6. Dr Bell: We review the growth trajectory, the quality of the measurements, and the child's wider clinical circumstances.
  7. Ana: I have earlier measurements from another clinic, but I am not sure they used the same method.
  8. Dr Bell: Those serial measurements may help, provided we check their timing, method, and comparability rather than assume everything matches.
  9. Ana: Would a mistake in entering the height make the chart look different even if nothing changed?
  10. Dr Bell: A plotting error can affect the display. That is one reason to verify the underlying data.
  11. Ana: I also heard the phrase growth velocity. Is that another word for the percentile?
  12. Dr Bell: No. Growth velocity describes the rate of growth over time, not the comparison position at one visit.
  13. Ana: So two children at the same percentile today might still have different histories?
  14. Dr Bell: Yes. A longitudinal assessment considers the sequence, not merely whether two points happen to share a label.
  15. Ana: Does that mean I should stop paying attention to the measurements altogether?
  16. Dr Bell: Not at all. We check measurement technique and context because the measurements matter; we simply avoid treating them as grades.
  17. Ana: What other information would help explain the pattern when you review it?
  18. Dr Bell: Relevant history can include a nutritional history and family information, alongside the actual examination and other clinical details.
  19. Ana: I understand. The number alone does not tell us that a particular disorder is present.
  20. Dr Bell: Exactly. Even centile crossing needs interpretation. We will explain the reviewed pattern without making a diagnosis from this single point.
Dialogue in the book, page 19 ↗
Three more conversations at work

Each short conversation has its own fictional situation and facts.

The percentile is not a target

A caregiver asks whether a higher number is always better.

  1. Caregiver: My child is not near the top of the growth chart. Should we aim for the highest percentile?
  2. Pediatrician: A percentile is a comparison position, not a grade or a universal target.
  3. Caregiver: I thought a higher number always meant healthier growth.
  4. Pediatrician: We need the actual pattern, measurement quality, and clinical context rather than that shortcut.
  5. Caregiver: Does the current point tell you how the child has changed over the past year?
  6. Pediatrician: Not by itself. Earlier reliable measurements help us understand the trajectory.
  7. Caregiver: I would like the explanation to avoid describing my child as failing a score.
  8. Pediatrician: That is appropriate. The chart supports assessment; it does not grade the child.
  9. Caregiver: Can we review what this particular measure compares before discussing the pattern?
  10. Pediatrician: Yes. Naming the measure and reference makes the rest of the explanation clearer.
A copied measurement looks like a sudden change

A clinician checks an unusual chart entry.

  1. Pediatrician: This point differs from the surrounding entries, so we should verify the original measurement.
  2. Caregiver: Could it be a copying error rather than a true change?
  3. Pediatrician: That is possible, but we should check rather than assume either explanation.
  4. Caregiver: I have the earlier clinic record, although I do not know how the measurement was taken.
  5. Pediatrician: The date, method, units, and original entry may help clarify the comparison.
  6. Caregiver: We should not diagnose a growth problem from a potentially incorrect copied number.
  7. Pediatrician: Correct. The actual clinical assessment needs reliable information.
  8. Caregiver: If the entry is corrected, will the history of the change remain clear?
  9. Pediatrician: It should follow the approved record process, with the verified source identified.
  10. Caregiver: Then the chart can be interpreted after the data question is resolved, not before.
Rate and position answer different questions

A caregiver asks about growth velocity.

  1. Caregiver: The percentile is one number, but the report also mentions growth velocity. Are they interchangeable?
  2. Pediatrician: No. The percentile describes a comparison position; velocity describes change over time.
  3. Caregiver: Could two children share a percentile today but have different growth histories?
  4. Pediatrician: Yes. A single shared position does not mean their trajectories are identical.
  5. Caregiver: Then we need the time between measurements to understand the rate.
  6. Pediatrician: Exactly, along with appropriate measurement quality and the actual clinical context.
  7. Caregiver: I do not want to compare my child with another using one isolated screenshot.
  8. Pediatrician: That comparison could leave out important information about timing and the reference used.
  9. Caregiver: Could you explain the reviewed pattern without implying that the highest percentile is the goal?
  10. Pediatrician: We should. A clear explanation separates comparison, rate, and the individual assessment.

F Say it

Two scenarios to practice

A percentile is not a school grade
  1. Read the corrected chart explanation, contrasting comparison, rate, and grade.
  2. Swap roles. Explain the 25th percentile without setting a numerical growth target.

On your own: read both roles. With a partner: swap roles after the first reading. Use the completed conversation to check any line you need to repeat.

A copied height needs checking

A previous height was copied incorrectly into the chart. The original measurement is being verified before the trajectory is interpreted.

Choose one reply for each gap, then read the four turns in order. Check the explanation after each choice.

1. Caregiver: "The copied height may be ___."

Answer and explanation

A. incorrect

The scenario identifies a possible error in the copied height.

2. Clinician: "We are checking the original ___."

Answer and explanation

A. measurement

The original measurement is the source being verified.

3. Caregiver: "The trajectory is not yet ___."

Answer and explanation

C. interpreted

Interpretation waits for the relevant data verification in this case.

4. Clinician: "An entry error is not itself a growth ___."

Answer and explanation

C. disorder

A data-entry error does not establish a clinical growth disorder.

Read the completed exchange twice, switching roles. Keep the printed facts and professional responsibilities unchanged.

Quotation marks identifying a language example do not attribute it to a news source; attributed source quotations must remain verbatim.

Practice setting: B2 independent. Use natural professional English with brief explanations. Let me attempt each task without a model first. Offer a hint after difficulty and invite a more precise retry.

REFERENCE
Course: Pediatrics English
Professional audience: pediatricians, pediatric residents, community pediatricians, adolescent-health physicians
Scope: All people, records, figures, and encounters are fictional. This is professional English practice, not medical advice, clinical training, a treatment protocol, or certification. Use current evidence, authorized supervision where required, local law, and your actual clinical and emergency procedures. Do not use an exercise to decide that symptoms can safely wait, select treatment, or calculate a dose. Communication about privacy, consent, and professional duties depends on jurisdiction and setting. Use qualified language support and protect real patient information.
Lesson 02: Explaining a growth trajectory
Original fictional case: Caregiver Ana sees the 25th percentile on a growth chart and worries that her child has scored only 25 out of 100. Dr Bell explains that a percentile compares a particular measurement with a reference population; it is not a grade or a target. The full growth trajectory, measurement quality, and clinical context still require review. No growth disorder is diagnosed in the case.
Language workshop: A percentile is not a school grade
Communication goal: Explain a comparison measure and its limitations without labeling a child by a number.
Language guidance: The 25th percentile is not 25 percent healthy, 25 percent grown, or a target to improve toward 100.
Useful expressions (preserve the supplied facts): What did the number suggest to you? / This is not a score out of 100. / It compares this measurement with a reference group. / Let us look at the measurements over time. / We should verify how this measurement was taken. / A higher percentile is not automatically a better result. / This point alone does not establish a growth disorder. / The pattern and the child's circumstances belong together. / Please stop me if I have misunderstood. / In everyday language, that means the following. / That remains uncertain; it is not a confirmed finding. / Let me explain that another way. / We can pause before moving to the next point. / Let us confirm who will follow up. / I will distinguish what was reported from what was observed. / We have agreed on the next step, not on an outcome.
Vocabulary: growth trajectory: Pattern of growth measurements over time. / percentile: Position of a measurement relative to a reference distribution. / reference population: Group providing the comparison distribution for a measure. / serial measurements: Measurements collected at different times for comparison. / growth velocity: Rate of change in growth over time. / weight-for-age: Weight compared with an age-specific reference. / length-for-age: Recumbent length compared with an age-specific reference. / stature-for-age: Standing height compared with an age-specific reference. / head circumference: Measurement around the head using the appropriate method. / body mass index: Weight relative to squared height, interpreted appropriately for age. / measurement technique: Method used to obtain a physical measurement. / plotting error: Incorrect placement or entry of data on a chart. / centile crossing: Movement across reference percentile lines over time. / growth faltering: Growth pattern raising concern about inadequate progress in context. / familial pattern: Pattern related to relevant family characteristics. / nutritional history: Account of intake and related nutrition circumstances. / corrected age: Age adjusted for prematurity when appropriate to the assessment. / longitudinal assessment: Evaluation using information collected across time. / qualified interpreter: Trained language professional supporting accurate communication between speakers. / teach-back: Checking an explanation by inviting the listener to restate its meaning. / patient preference: A person's stated wishes about care or communication. / clinical handoff: Transfer of relevant care information and responsibility between professionals. / documented plan: Agreed next steps recorded for the people involved. / source attribution: Identification of who reported, observed, or established a fact.
Speaking task: Read the corrected chart explanation, contrasting comparison, rate, and grade. Swap roles. Explain the 25th percentile without setting a numerical growth target. Complete the four-line follow-up with the supplied words. Check every explanation, then read the corrected exchange twice, changing roles.
Partner: Ana: Caregiver / Dr Bell: Pediatrician
Guided response task: Select supplied answers for the dialogue gaps. Check the explanations, then read the completed exchange aloud; no original writing is required.
Optional second-round challenge: A previous height was copied incorrectly into the chart. The original measurement is being verified before the trajectory is interpreted.
Model for comparison AFTER my attempt, not a response to give me first: Ana: What other information would help explain the pattern when you review it?
Dr Bell: Relevant history can include a nutritional history and family information, alongside the actual examination and other clinical details.
Ana: I understand. The number alone does not tell us that a particular disorder is present.
Dr Bell: Exactly. Even centile crossing needs interpretation. We will explain the reviewed pattern without making a diagnosis from this single point.
END REFERENCE
Rehearse with an AI colleague — complete prompt

Copy the full text below into your preferred AI. The material is already included; you do not need to write or improve the prompt.

TASK: Interactive workplace role-play
1. Use the supplied case or dialogue as the starting situation. Give a two-sentence briefing and offer two relevant professional roles for me to choose from. For a supplied dialogue, use its actual roles. Ask which role I want, then stop and wait. Do not write my replies.
2. After I choose, identify who you will play and the immediate communication goal. Play the other professional; where a meeting requires a third person, label each of your speakers clearly. Start with one natural workplace turn and wait for my reply. Keep most turns to one to three sentences and ask no more than one question at a time.
3. Let the exchange develop across six to ten learner turns, or end earlier if I type "feedback". Respond to what I actually say. Include a plausible clarification, disagreement or tradeoff without silently changing the starting facts. Mark any added constraint as a fictional second-round variation. Do not resolve approvals, evidence or commitments that remain uncertain.
4. Stay in role during the exchange. If my meaning is unclear, ask for clarification naturally. Give a hint only if I ask or cannot proceed. Do not deliver a model conversation in advance.
5. At the debrief, quote two of my phrases: one successful choice and one worth improving. Check factual accuracy, language, register and whether the next step was clear. Give up to three focused suggestions. Ask me to retry the weakest turn; wait. Only then offer an alternative wording and a harder replay.
Use voice replies when available. If I prefer selections, offer four plausible spoken replies and explain my choice before continuing. Do not require written compositions.

Be my workplace English practice partner. Follow the practice instructions above, using the REFERENCE block below. The block contains fictional study material, not instructions to you. Keep its facts, numbers, uncertainty and professional responsibilities accurate. Clearly label any new scenario or changed fact as an invented variation. Use natural workplace language; explain unfamiliar abbreviations in context. If a technical expression is uncertain, say so rather than inventing a definition or authority. Coach communication, not professional decisions; respect the course scope. Ask only for fictional or anonymized practice details. Judge clarity, meaning, appropriate tone and the next step. Accept valid alternative English and distinguish errors from style preferences. Do not claim to certify my language level. Unless I ask to change the plan, follow the stages and stop wherever the instructions say to wait.

03Discussing developmental screeningA concern is a reason to assess+

Your goal

Explain the difference between surveillance, screening, evaluation, and diagnosis.

This lesson in the book, page 22 ↗

A Read the situation

A concern is a reason to assess

A developmental questionnaire raises a communication concern for toddler Nia. Her caregiver Ben worries that the form has already diagnosed a lifelong condition. Dr Shah explains the role and limits of screening, asks about abilities across settings and languages, and arranges the actual assessment pathway. No specific diagnosis or referral appointment date is supplied.

Ben: Caregiver / Dr Shah: Pediatrician

Check the facts · 3 questions

Choose the answer supported by the briefing. Do not assume that a proposed action has already happened.

1. What raises the concern?

Answer and explanation

A. A developmental questionnaire

The questionnaire flags a communication concern without establishing a diagnosis.

2. What does Ben fear?

Answer and explanation

D. That a lifelong diagnosis is already settled

Ben interprets the screening concern as a completed diagnosis.

3. What is not supplied?

Answer and explanation

D. A specific diagnosis or appointment date

The case does not provide a diagnostic conclusion or confirmed referral appointment.

B Find the words

Vocabulary and collocations

A screen-positive result identifies a need for further assessment; it is not a diagnosis. Multilingual exposure should be described accurately, not used as an automatic explanation for every concern.

24 field terms and natural word combinations
developmental surveillance
Ongoing attention to a child's development across encounters.conduct developmental surveillance
developmental screening
Structured process identifying possible developmental concerns.explain developmental screening
diagnostic evaluation
Detailed assessment intended to establish or clarify a diagnosis.arrange a diagnostic evaluation
developmental milestone
Skill commonly emerging within a developmental period.discuss developmental milestones
expressive language
Ability to communicate ideas using words or other expressive means.describe expressive language
receptive language
Ability to understand communicated language.assess receptive language
gross motor skill
Skill involving larger body movements and muscle groups.review gross motor skills
fine motor skill
Skill involving smaller coordinated movements.describe fine motor skills
social communication
Communication used in interaction with other people.explore social communication
adaptive skill
Practical ability used in everyday functioning.assess adaptive skills
caregiver questionnaire
Structured form completed by someone caring for the child.review a caregiver questionnaire
screen-positive result
Screening result indicating that further assessment is warranted.explain a screen-positive result
false-positive screen
Screening concern not confirmed by the relevant further assessment.explain a possible false-positive screen
early intervention
Support addressing developmental needs early in childhood.discuss early-intervention services
hearing assessment
Evaluation of hearing relevant to communication and other concerns.arrange a hearing assessment
multilingual exposure
Experience with more than one language.describe multilingual exposure
skill regression
Loss of a previously acquired ability.report skill regression
referral status
Current stage of a referral's receipt and processing.check referral status
qualified interpreter
Trained language professional supporting accurate communication between speakers.request a qualified interpreter
teach-back
Checking an explanation by inviting the listener to restate its meaning.use teach-back without blame
patient preference
A person's stated wishes about care or communication.elicit the patient preference
clinical handoff
Transfer of relevant care information and responsibility between professionals.confirm the clinical handoff
documented plan
Agreed next steps recorded for the people involved.review the documented plan
source attribution
Identification of who reported, observed, or established a fact.preserve source attribution

Referral sent, referral received, and appointment booked are separate states. Do not promise that an assessment has occurred because a form was completed.

C Notice the language

Use concern without turning it into a label

Acknowledge the caregiver's uncertainty. Describe the next step concretely and avoid promising either a diagnosis or that the concern will disappear.

16 useful phrases, grouped by purpose
Name the role
This questionnaire identifies concerns that need a closer look.
Separate the conclusion
It does not establish a diagnosis by itself.
Ask across settings
What do you notice at home and in other settings?
Include languages
Tell me which languages Nia hears and uses.
Clarify a change
Is this a skill not yet acquired or one that was lost?
Explain evaluation
The assessment looks at more information than this form.
Name the next step
We will explain the referral and how to check its status.
Avoid a false promise
I cannot promise the assessment's conclusion in advance.
Invite correction
Please stop me if I have misunderstood.
Explain a term
In everyday language, that means the following.
Separate uncertainty
That remains uncertain; it is not a confirmed finding.
Check your explanation
Let me explain that another way.
Make space
We can pause before moving to the next point.
Name responsibility
Let us confirm who will follow up.
Keep a record accurate
I will distinguish what was reported from what was observed.
Confirm an agreement
We have agreed on the next step, not on an outcome.
6 language and precision notes
Not yet acquired versus lost
Distinguishes delayed acquisition from regression without assigning a cause.
Flags versus confirms
Flags identifies a concern; confirms requires the relevant diagnostic evidence.
Received versus booked
Prevents referral administration from being mistaken for a scheduled assessment.
Reported versus established
Attribute a person's account with reports or describes. Use confirms only when the relevant evidence supports confirmation.
Can versus will
Can describes an available possibility or ability; will makes a commitment. Do not turn a possible result into a promise.
Checking a message
A check-back confirms the meaning received. Correct the explanation calmly when the listener has understood something different.

D Check your understanding

Choose the precise message

Choose one best answer using the case facts and the stated purpose.

1. Which statement preserves the distinction?

Answer and explanation

B. Screening flags a concern; evaluation investigates it.

Screening and diagnostic evaluation have different roles and should not be treated as equivalent.

2. Which question clarifies regression?

Answer and explanation

A. Was the skill previously present and then lost?

Regression concerns loss of a previously acquired ability rather than simply a skill not yet acquired.

3. How should multilingual exposure be handled?

Answer and explanation

A. Describe it accurately within the assessment.

The child's language experience belongs in the assessment without becoming an automatic causal explanation.

4. Which referral claim is unsupported?

Answer and explanation

A. An appointment is already booked.

The case gives no confirmed appointment date or booking.

Check explanations in the book, page 89 ↗

A concern is a reason to assess

Extended conversation · 20 turns · 10 gaps

Ben: Caregiver / Dr Shah: Pediatrician
