    The questionnaire flagged a communication concern. Has it already diagnosed Nia with something that will last her whole life?
  2. Dr Shah
    No. 1 identifies concerns for a closer look; it does not settle a diagnosis by itself.
  3. Ben
    I thought the score meant the assessment had already been completed and the conclusion was final.
  4. Dr Shah
    A 2 considers more information, including history, observation, and the relevant assessments.
  5. Ben
    What kinds of language skills are you asking about? She understands some things she does not say.
  6. Dr Shah
    3 concerns understanding, while expressive language concerns what she communicates. We should distinguish those accounts.
  7. Ben
    She hears two languages at home and uses different words with different relatives.
  8. Dr Shah
    Please describe that 4 accurately. We should not assume it explains every concern or ignore abilities in another language.
  9. Ben
    Some questions also asked about pointing and how she interacts with other children.
  10. Dr Shah
    Those can relate to 5, which is broader than counting spoken words alone.
  11. Ben
    If she has never used a particular word, is that the same as losing a skill?
  12. Dr Shah
    No. 6 means an ability was present and then lost. The distinction matters in the history.
  13. Ben
    Could hearing be part of the assessment, even if we think she hears some sounds?
  14. Dr Shah
    A relevant 7 may form part of the actual pathway. An informal impression does not replace the appropriate assessment.
  15. Ben
    Does getting support have to mean that every diagnostic question has already been answered?
  16. Dr Shah
    We can discuss appropriate 8 and the local process while explaining what remains uncertain.
  17. Ben
    I would like to know whom to contact if we do not hear about the referral.
  18. Dr Shah
    We will explain how to check 9, including the difference between sending a request and having a confirmed appointment.
  19. Ben
    That gives us a next step without pretending the questionnaire has answered everything.
  20. Dr Shah
    Exactly. Ongoing 10 continues alongside the appropriate assessment and communication with your family.

Answers and explanations
  1. Developmental screening Turn 2. Developmental screening flags concerns for assessment; it does not establish diagnosis.
  2. diagnostic evaluation Turn 4. A diagnostic evaluation examines additional information rather than treating screening as definitive.
  3. Receptive language Turn 6. Receptive language concerns understanding, whereas expressive language concerns communicating ideas.
  4. multilingual exposure Turn 8. Multilingual exposure belongs in the history but does not explain every concern.
  5. social communication Turn 10. Social communication includes interaction and is broader than counting spoken words.
  6. Skill regression Turn 12. Skill regression means loss of a previously acquired ability and differs from a skill that has not yet emerged.
  7. hearing assessment Turn 14. A hearing assessment evaluates hearing and may contribute to investigating communication concerns rather than being replaced by an informal impression.
  8. early intervention Turn 16. Early intervention addresses developmental needs early in childhood, with actual eligibility and services determined through the relevant local process.
  9. referral status Turn 18. Referral status identifies the current processing stage and should distinguish a sent referral from receipt or a booked appointment.
  10. developmental surveillance Turn 20. Developmental surveillance follows development across encounters and complements, rather than replaces, screening and appropriate diagnostic assessment.
Read the complete conversation
  1. Ben: The questionnaire flagged a communication concern. Has it already diagnosed Nia with something that will last her whole life?
  2. Dr Shah: No. Developmental screening identifies concerns for a closer look; it does not settle a diagnosis by itself.
  3. Ben: I thought the score meant the assessment had already been completed and the conclusion was final.
  4. Dr Shah: A diagnostic evaluation considers more information, including history, observation, and the relevant assessments.
  5. Ben: What kinds of language skills are you asking about? She understands some things she does not say.
  6. Dr Shah: Receptive language concerns understanding, while expressive language concerns what she communicates. We should distinguish those accounts.
  7. Ben: She hears two languages at home and uses different words with different relatives.
  8. Dr Shah: Please describe that multilingual exposure accurately. We should not assume it explains every concern or ignore abilities in another language.
  9. Ben: Some questions also asked about pointing and how she interacts with other children.
  10. Dr Shah: Those can relate to social communication, which is broader than counting spoken words alone.
  11. Ben: If she has never used a particular word, is that the same as losing a skill?
  12. Dr Shah: No. Skill regression means an ability was present and then lost. The distinction matters in the history.
  13. Ben: Could hearing be part of the assessment, even if we think she hears some sounds?
  14. Dr Shah: A relevant hearing assessment may form part of the actual pathway. An informal impression does not replace the appropriate assessment.
  15. Ben: Does getting support have to mean that every diagnostic question has already been answered?
  16. Dr Shah: We can discuss appropriate early intervention and the local process while explaining what remains uncertain.
  17. Ben: I would like to know whom to contact if we do not hear about the referral.
  18. Dr Shah: We will explain how to check referral status, including the difference between sending a request and having a confirmed appointment.
  19. Ben: That gives us a next step without pretending the questionnaire has answered everything.
  20. Dr Shah: Exactly. Ongoing developmental surveillance continues alongside the appropriate assessment and communication with your family.
Dialogue in the book, page 28 ↗
Three more conversations at work

Each short conversation has its own fictional situation and facts.

A screen-positive result is not a diagnosis

A caregiver asks what a questionnaire establishes.

  1. Caregiver: The form flagged a concern, and I thought that meant a diagnosis had already been made.
  2. Pediatrician: Screening identifies a reason for further assessment; it does not settle the diagnosis by itself.
  3. Caregiver: Does that mean we should ignore the result because it is not definitive?
  4. Pediatrician: No. It informs the next step, which we should explain clearly.
  5. Caregiver: I want to know what the assessment will explore rather than receive a label without context.
  6. Pediatrician: The actual pathway considers relevant history, observations, and appropriate evaluation.
  7. Caregiver: Could you also explain who checks whether the referral has been received?
  8. Pediatrician: We should identify that responsibility and distinguish receipt from a booked appointment.
  9. Caregiver: Then the concern has a concrete next step without a promised diagnostic outcome.
  10. Pediatrician: Exactly. Neither a guarantee of normality nor a premature diagnosis would be appropriate.
Not yet acquired and lost are different

A clinician clarifies a developmental history.

  1. Pediatrician: When you say the skill is missing, was it previously present and then lost?
  2. Caregiver: No. I mean the child has not yet shown it, not that it disappeared.
  3. Pediatrician: Thank you. Delayed acquisition and regression describe different histories.
  4. Caregiver: I had used the word regression because I thought it meant any developmental concern.
  5. Pediatrician: We should correct the meaning without assuming a cause from either term.
  6. Caregiver: Could the record distinguish what I saw from what another caregiver reported?
  7. Pediatrician: Yes. The source and circumstances of each account should remain clear.
  8. Caregiver: I also want the child's abilities in different settings included.
  9. Pediatrician: That context belongs in the actual assessment rather than a conclusion from one word.
  10. Caregiver: Then the referral can carry an accurate history instead of a label I used incorrectly.
More than one language belongs in the history

A caregiver asks how multilingual experience is considered.

  1. Caregiver: The child uses different words with different relatives, and I worry the form counted only English.
  2. Pediatrician: We need an accurate account of the languages heard and used across settings.
  3. Caregiver: Does speaking more than one language automatically explain every communication concern?
  4. Pediatrician: No. It should be considered properly, not used as a universal explanation.
  5. Caregiver: I can describe understanding in one language and expression in another.
  6. Pediatrician: That helps distinguish receptive and expressive abilities rather than count only one kind of response.
  7. Caregiver: Could qualified language support help us describe the history accurately?
  8. Pediatrician: Yes, where needed, so a language barrier does not distort the assessment.
  9. Caregiver: I do not want abilities ignored or a diagnosis assumed from multilingual exposure alone.
  10. Pediatrician: Neither would be appropriate. The actual developmental evaluation needs the relevant full context.

F Say it

Two scenarios to practice

A concern is a reason to assess
  1. Read the corrected screening discussion, keeping each assessment stage distinct.
  2. Swap roles. Explain the next step without predicting a diagnosis or an appointment date.

On your own: read both roles. With a partner: swap roles after the first reading. Use the completed conversation to check any line you need to repeat.

Receipt is confirmed, booking is not

The developmental service confirms receipt of Nia's referral. No appointment has been booked. The family has a contact for checking progress.

Choose one reply for each gap, then read the four turns in order. Check the explanation after each choice.

1. Caregiver: "The service has confirmed ___."

Answer and explanation

B. receipt

The supplied update confirms that the referral reached the service.

2. Clinician: "An appointment is not yet ___."

Answer and explanation

D. booked

Receipt does not establish that an appointment has been scheduled.

3. Caregiver: "We have a progress-check ___."

Answer and explanation

D. contact

The scenario provides a contact for checking the referral's progress.

4. Clinician: "The diagnostic conclusion remains ___."

Answer and explanation

B. pending

No completed diagnostic assessment or conclusion is supplied in this update.

Read the completed exchange twice, switching roles. Keep the printed facts and professional responsibilities unchanged.

Practice grammar in context — complete prompt

Copy the full text below into your preferred AI. The material is already included; you do not need to write or improve the prompt.

TASK: Choose grammar that preserves the meaning
1. Use the lesson's language workshop, or one actual sentence from the dialogue, to explain a single grammar distinction in no more than 90 words. Show how it affects timing, certainty, responsibility or politeness.
2. Give one brief occupational exchange containing a gap and four complete options A-D. Supply enough context for exactly one answer. Keep distractors plausible; distinguish grammar errors from wording that changes the facts. Stop and wait.
3. Explain why each choice fits or fails, then ask me to read the completed exchange aloud. Do not request a draft, original written sentence or essay.
4. Repeat with three new exchanges, one at a time, moving from recognition to a more subtle meaning contrast. Do not copy the published checks. Label invented variations and do not turn uncertainty into a promise.
5. End with a short contrast table and two supported spoken turns using supplied frames. Accept accurate alternatives in oral practice; acknowledge and replace ambiguous multiple-choice items.

Be my workplace English practice partner. Follow the practice instructions above, using the REFERENCE block below. The block contains fictional study material, not instructions to you. Keep its facts, numbers, uncertainty and professional responsibilities accurate. Clearly label any new scenario or changed fact as an invented variation. Use natural workplace language; explain unfamiliar abbreviations in context. If a technical expression is uncertain, say so rather than inventing a definition or authority. Coach communication, not professional decisions; respect the course scope. Ask only for fictional or anonymized practice details. Judge clarity, meaning, appropriate tone and the next step. Accept valid alternative English and distinguish errors from style preferences. Do not claim to certify my language level. Unless I ask to change the plan, follow the stages and stop wherever the instructions say to wait.

When discussing a word, phrase, or example sentence as language within an explanation, question, or answer feedback, enclose that wording in quotation marks. For example: What does “about” mean in “about twenty people”? Use “on” with a named day. Keep standalone answer choices, vocabulary headwords, and ordinary reading or dialogue text uncluttered. Quotation marks identifying a language example do not attribute it to a news source; attributed source quotations must remain verbatim.

TASK: Interactive workplace role-play
1. Use the supplied case or dialogue as the starting situation. Give a two-sentence briefing and offer two relevant professional roles for me to choose from. For a supplied dialogue, use its actual roles. Ask which role I want, then stop and wait. Do not write my replies.
2. After I choose, identify who you will play and the immediate communication goal. Play the other professional; where a meeting requires a third person, label each of your speakers clearly. Start with one natural workplace turn and wait for my reply. Keep most turns to one to three sentences and ask no more than one question at a time.
3. Let the exchange develop across six to ten learner turns, or end earlier if I type "feedback". Respond to what I actually say. Include a plausible clarification, disagreement or tradeoff without silently changing the starting facts. Mark any added constraint as a fictional second-round variation. Do not resolve approvals, evidence or commitments that remain uncertain.
4. Stay in role during the exchange. If my meaning is unclear, ask for clarification naturally. Give a hint only if I ask or cannot proceed. Do not deliver a model conversation in advance.
5. At the debrief, quote two of my phrases: one successful choice and one worth improving. Check factual accuracy, language, register and whether the next step was clear. Give up to three focused suggestions. Ask me to retry the weakest turn; wait. Only then offer an alternative wording and a harder replay.
Use voice replies when available. If I prefer selections, offer four plausible spoken replies and explain my choice before continuing. Do not require written compositions.

04Responding to vaccine questionsA concern deserves a specific answer+

Your goal

Elicit the actual concern and explain a recommendation without shaming or claiming zero risk.

This lesson in the book, page 31 ↗

A Read the situation

A concern deserves a specific answer

Caregiver Dana worries about fever after a previous vaccination and asks whether the same thing will happen again. Dr Lin asks what occurred and when, reviews the actual record, and explains that timing alone does not establish causation. The clinician will review the current recommendation and individual history. No vaccine schedule, eligibility decision, or product-specific risk estimate is supplied.

Dana: Caregiver / Dr Lin: Pediatrician

Check the facts · 3 questions

Choose the answer supported by the briefing. Do not assume that a proposed action has already happened.

1. What worries Dana?

Answer and explanation

A. Fever after a previous vaccination

Dana asks about fever after a previous vaccination and whether it will recur.

2. What needs review?

Answer and explanation

A. The event history and actual recommendation

The clinician reviews the individual account and current recommendation rather than assuming an answer.

3. What is not supplied?

Answer and explanation

D. A product-specific risk estimate

No product-specific estimate is available in this language case.

B Find the words

Vocabulary and collocations

After vaccination describes timing. Caused by vaccination requires a causal assessment. Do not dismiss the event or declare its cause without review.

24 field terms and natural word combinations
immunization
Process of developing protection against a disease, commonly through vaccination.discuss immunization
vaccine recommendation
Current advice about use of a particular vaccine in context.review the vaccine recommendation
vaccination history
Record of previous vaccine administration.verify vaccination history
adverse event
Unwanted occurrence after an intervention, not necessarily caused by it.document an adverse event
adverse reaction
Harmful response attributed to an intervention.distinguish an adverse reaction
temporal association
Events occurring in a time relationship without proving causation.explain temporal association
causal relationship
Connection in which one event contributes to producing another.assess a causal relationship
contraindication
Condition making a particular intervention inappropriate under applicable guidance.verify a contraindication
precaution
Circumstance requiring additional consideration before an intervention.review a relevant precaution
expected reaction
Known response discussed in the context of a specific product.explain an expected reaction
vaccine hesitancy
Uncertainty or reluctance concerning vaccination.explore vaccine hesitancy
informed choice
Decision made after relevant information and questions are addressed.support informed choice
disease protection
Reduction in disease risk or consequences through an intervention.explain disease protection
breakthrough infection
Infection occurring despite relevant vaccination or protection.discuss breakthrough infection
catch-up schedule
Recommended sequence addressing previously missed vaccine doses.verify a catch-up schedule
administration record
Documentation of a product given and its relevant details.check the administration record
safety monitoring
Ongoing observation and review of potential safety issues.explain safety monitoring
absolute risk
Probability or frequency of an outcome in a defined group and period.communicate absolute risk
qualified interpreter
Trained language professional supporting accurate communication between speakers.request a qualified interpreter
teach-back
Checking an explanation by inviting the listener to restate its meaning.use teach-back without blame
patient preference
A person's stated wishes about care or communication.elicit the patient preference
clinical handoff
Transfer of relevant care information and responsibility between professionals.confirm the clinical handoff
documented plan
Agreed next steps recorded for the people involved.review the documented plan
source attribution
Identification of who reported, observed, or established a fact.preserve source attribution

Use current, jurisdiction-appropriate recommendations and the actual clinical history. This exercise supplies no schedule, contraindication decision, or guarantee of no reaction.

C Notice the language

Specific questions beat labels

Ask what the caregiver observed and fears. Labels such as difficult or anti-science obscure the question and can damage the discussion.

16 useful phrases, grouped by purpose
Find the concern
What happened after the previous vaccination?
Clarify timing
How soon afterward did you notice the change?
Acknowledge the experience
I can understand why that experience raised a question.
Separate cause and timing
Happening afterward does not by itself establish the cause.
Review the record
Let us check the actual administration record.
Avoid zero-risk language
I will explain the relevant benefits and possible harms.
Invite a focused question
Which part of the recommendation remains unclear?
Close with understanding
Please tell me how you understand the next step.
Invite correction
Please stop me if I have misunderstood.
Explain a term
In everyday language, that means the following.
Separate uncertainty
That remains uncertain; it is not a confirmed finding.
Check your explanation
Let me explain that another way.
Make space
We can pause before moving to the next point.
Name responsibility
Let us confirm who will follow up.
Keep a record accurate
I will distinguish what was reported from what was observed.
Confirm an agreement
We have agreed on the next step, not on an outcome.
6 language and precision notes
After versus because of
After states sequence; because of assigns a cause.
May recur versus will recur
A possible recurrence is not a prediction that the event is certain.
Current recommendation
Keeps the discussion tied to applicable guidance rather than a remembered universal schedule.
Reported versus established
Attribute a person's account with reports or describes. Use confirms only when the relevant evidence supports confirmation.
Can versus will
Can describes an available possibility or ability; will makes a commitment. Do not turn a possible result into a promise.
Checking a message
A check-back confirms the meaning received. Correct the explanation calmly when the listener has understood something different.

D Check your understanding

Choose the precise message

Choose one best answer using the case facts and the stated purpose.

1. Which wording preserves causation uncertainty?

Answer and explanation

A. Fever occurred after vaccination; its cause needs review.

The wording acknowledges the event while keeping the causal question open for review.

2. Which claim is unsupported?

Answer and explanation

B. No reaction can ever occur.

A universal zero-risk promise cannot be justified by this scenario.

3. What is an adverse event?

Answer and explanation

C. An unwanted occurrence not necessarily caused by the intervention

An event after an intervention and a causally attributed reaction are not automatically identical.

4. Which question is most useful first?

Answer and explanation

D. What happened and when?

Specific event and timing details support assessment more effectively than labeling the caregiver.

Check explanations in the book, page 91 ↗

E Conversations

A concern deserves a specific answer

Extended conversation · 20 turns · 10 gaps

Dana: Caregiver / Dr Lin: Pediatrician

Complete the numbered gaps. Use each bank entry once, without changing its form.

Word bank
  • contraindication
  • informed choice
  • catch-up schedule
  • vaccination history
  • temporal association
  • causal relationship
  • adverse event
  • administration record
  • absolute risk
  • vaccine recommendation
  1. Dana
    My child had a fever after the last vaccination. Does that mean the same thing will happen again?
  2. Dr Lin
    Let us first review the 1 and what you noticed, including when it started and what happened afterward.
  3. Dana
    I appreciate that. I was worried you would think asking about it meant refusing every vaccine.
  4. Dr Lin
    Asking a question is not a blanket refusal. Your account helps us review the relevant 2 rather than dismiss it.
  5. Dana
    The timing seemed very close, so I assumed that was enough to prove the cause.
  6. Dr Lin
    A 3 tells us when things happened. It does not, by itself, settle the cause.
  7. Dana
    So you are not saying the fever did not happen, only that its explanation needs review?
  8. Dr Lin
    Exactly. A 4 is a separate question from whether you observed an event.
  9. Dana
    What if something in the medical history means a particular vaccine should not be given?
  10. Dr Lin
    We review any relevant 5 and precautions using the actual history and current guidance.
  11. Dana
    I would like to hear the benefit as well as what unwanted effects are possible.
  12. Dr Lin
    The 6 needs that discussion. I should not replace it with a promise of zero risk.
  13. Dana
    Sometimes a percentage is hard to understand without knowing how many children it refers to.
  14. Dr Lin
    When relevant figures are available, 7 needs the outcome, group, and time period made clear.
  15. Dana
    I also have a record from another clinic, but I am unsure whether every entry was copied.
  16. Dr Lin
    We should verify the 8 rather than assume a copied list is complete.
  17. Dana
    Does that mean you will create a catch-up plan just from what I remember today?
  18. Dr Lin
    No. Any 9 requires the verified history and applicable recommendation, not a guessed sequence.
  19. Dana
    That helps me ask a more specific question instead of trying to decide everything from one memory.
  20. Dr Lin
    Good. An 10 depends on clear information and a chance to resolve the questions that matter to you.

Answers and explanations
  1. vaccination history Turn 2. Vaccination history records prior administrations and supports review of the reported event.
  2. adverse event Turn 4. An adverse event occurs after intervention without necessarily being caused by it.
  3. temporal association Turn 6. Temporal association establishes sequence, not proof that one event caused another.
  4. causal relationship Turn 8. A causal relationship needs assessment beyond merely identifying which event happened first.
  5. contraindication Turn 10. A contraindication requires verification against the actual history and applicable guidance.
  6. vaccine recommendation Turn 12. A vaccine recommendation is current advice for a product and context and should be explained with its relevant benefits, limitations, and risks.
  7. absolute risk Turn 14. Absolute risk gives the probability or frequency for a defined outcome, group, and period, rather than a context-free percentage.
  8. administration record Turn 16. An administration record documents what was actually given and should be checked before missing entries are treated as completed or omitted doses.
  9. catch-up schedule Turn 18. A catch-up schedule addresses missed doses under current guidance and requires the actual verified history rather than an improvised sequence.
  10. informed choice Turn 20. An informed choice follows relevant information and questions being addressed rather than pressure to agree before the concern is understood.
Read the complete conversation
  1. Dana: My child had a fever after the last vaccination. Does that mean the same thing will happen again?
  2. Dr Lin: Let us first review the vaccination history and what you noticed, including when it started and what happened afterward.
  3. Dana: I appreciate that. I was worried you would think asking about it meant refusing every vaccine.
  4. Dr Lin: Asking a question is not a blanket refusal. Your account helps us review the relevant adverse event rather than dismiss it.
  5. Dana: The timing seemed very close, so I assumed that was enough to prove the cause.
  6. Dr Lin: A temporal association tells us when things happened. It does not, by itself, settle the cause.
  7. Dana: So you are not saying the fever did not happen, only that its explanation needs review?
  8. Dr Lin: Exactly. A causal relationship is a separate question from whether you observed an event.
  9. Dana: What if something in the medical history means a particular vaccine should not be given?
  10. Dr Lin: We review any relevant contraindication and precautions using the actual history and current guidance.
  11. Dana: I would like to hear the benefit as well as what unwanted effects are possible.
  12. Dr Lin: The vaccine recommendation needs that discussion. I should not replace it with a promise of zero risk.
  13. Dana: Sometimes a percentage is hard to understand without knowing how many children it refers to.
  14. Dr Lin: When relevant figures are available, absolute risk needs the outcome, group, and time period made clear.
  15. Dana: I also have a record from another clinic, but I am unsure whether every entry was copied.
  16. Dr Lin: We should verify the administration record rather than assume a copied list is complete.
  17. Dana: Does that mean you will create a catch-up plan just from what I remember today?
  18. Dr Lin: No. Any catch-up schedule requires the verified history and applicable recommendation, not a guessed sequence.
  19. Dana: That helps me ask a more specific question instead of trying to decide everything from one memory.
  20. Dr Lin: Good. An informed choice depends on clear information and a chance to resolve the questions that matter to you.
Dialogue in the book, page 37 ↗
Three more conversations at work

Each short conversation has its own fictional situation and facts.

After does not prove because of

A caregiver asks about an event following vaccination.

  1. Caregiver: A symptom occurred after the vaccination, so I assumed the vaccination definitely caused it.
  2. Pediatrician: The timing matters, but sequence alone does not establish the cause.
  3. Caregiver: I do not want that distinction used to deny that the symptom happened.
  4. Pediatrician: It should not be. We can acknowledge the event while reviewing its explanation.
  5. Caregiver: What information would help the actual assessment?
  6. Pediatrician: The product record, timing, symptoms, and relevant clinical history need appropriate review.
  7. Caregiver: I cannot remember every detail, so I would rather say unsure than invent it.
  8. Pediatrician: That is more accurate than adding false precision to the account.
  9. Caregiver: Then we can discuss today's recommendation without automatically copying a conclusion from the earlier event.
  10. Pediatrician: Yes. The current decision needs the actual history and applicable clinical guidance.
A specific worry instead of a label

A clinician invites a focused vaccine question.

  1. Pediatrician: Which part of the recommendation worries you most?
  2. Caregiver: I am worried about the previous reaction, not opposed to every possible vaccination.
  3. Pediatrician: That distinction matters, and I will not replace your concern with a broad label.
  4. Caregiver: I want the benefit and possible harm explained using the actual product information.
  5. Pediatrician: We should discuss those in context rather than promise zero risk.
  6. Caregiver: Could you also explain what is known and what remains uncertain about the earlier event?
  7. Pediatrician: Yes. A clear account separates an observed event from a confirmed causal reaction.
  8. Caregiver: I would like time to ask a focused question before deciding.
  9. Pediatrician: Questions belong in an informed discussion, with actual timing and clinical requirements explained.
  10. Caregiver: Then the conversation can address the concern instead of turning it into an argument about my character.
An unclear record does not prove a missed dose

A caregiver brings an outside vaccination record.

  1. Caregiver: This outside record has an abbreviation I cannot read. Does that mean the dose was missed?
  2. Pediatrician: An unclear entry does not establish either that it was given or that it was missed.
  3. Caregiver: We should ask the originating service to clarify rather than fill in the history by guesswork.
  4. Pediatrician: Exactly. The actual administration record needs verification.
  5. Caregiver: Could a catch-up plan be selected before that uncertainty is resolved?
  6. Pediatrician: Any plan requires the relevant clinical review and current guidance, not an assumption from the unclear entry.
  7. Caregiver: I also want the date and product checked, not only the presence of a mark.
  8. Pediatrician: Those details can matter to interpreting the record accurately.
  9. Caregiver: Then we can keep the entry unverified while the appropriate clarification is obtained.
  10. Pediatrician: Yes. Unknown should remain unknown until the supporting information is available.

F Say it

Two scenarios to practice

A concern deserves a specific answer
  1. Read the corrected exchange. Distinguish timing from a causal claim.
  2. Swap roles. Acknowledge the concern without shaming, prescribing a schedule, or promising zero risk.

On your own: read both roles. With a partner: swap roles after the first reading. Use the completed conversation to check any line you need to repeat.

An incomplete outside record

An outside vaccination record contains an unclear entry. The clinic requests clarification before treating it as a confirmed administration.

Choose one reply for each gap, then read the four turns in order. Check the explanation after each choice.

1. Caregiver: "This entry is ___."

Answer and explanation

D. unclear

The outside record contains an entry whose meaning is unresolved.

2. Clinician: "We have requested ___."

Answer and explanation

D. clarification

The clinic asks the originating source to clarify the entry.

3. Caregiver: "Administration is not yet ___."

Answer and explanation

C. confirmed

An unclear entry is not proof that the vaccine was administered.

4. Clinician: "We will use the verified ___."

Answer and explanation

A. record

The next decision uses the clarified actual record rather than an assumption.

Read the completed exchange twice, switching roles. Keep the printed facts and professional responsibilities unchanged.

Lesson 04: Responding to vaccine questions
Original fictional case: Caregiver Dana worries about fever after a previous vaccination and asks whether the same thing will happen again. Dr Lin asks what occurred and when, reviews the actual record, and explains that timing alone does not establish causation. The clinician will review the current recommendation and individual history. No vaccine schedule, eligibility decision, or product-specific risk estimate is supplied.
Language workshop: A concern deserves a specific answer
Communication goal: Elicit the actual concern and explain a recommendation without shaming or claiming zero risk.
Language guidance: After vaccination describes timing. Caused by vaccination requires a causal assessment. Do not dismiss the event or declare its cause without review.
Useful expressions (preserve the supplied facts): What happened after the previous vaccination? / How soon afterward did you notice the change? / I can understand why that experience raised a question. / Happening afterward does not by itself establish the cause. / Let us check the actual administration record. / I will explain the relevant benefits and possible harms. / Which part of the recommendation remains unclear? / Please tell me how you understand the next step. / Please stop me if I have misunderstood. / In everyday language, that means the following. / That remains uncertain; it is not a confirmed finding. / Let me explain that another way. / We can pause before moving to the next point. / Let us confirm who will follow up. / I will distinguish what was reported from what was observed. / We have agreed on the next step, not on an outcome.
Vocabulary: immunization: Process of developing protection against a disease, commonly through vaccination. / vaccine recommendation: Current advice about use of a particular vaccine in context. / vaccination history: Record of previous vaccine administration. / adverse event: Unwanted occurrence after an intervention, not necessarily caused by it. / adverse reaction: Harmful response attributed to an intervention. / temporal association: Events occurring in a time relationship without proving causation. / causal relationship: Connection in which one event contributes to producing another. / contraindication: Condition making a particular intervention inappropriate under applicable guidance. / precaution: Circumstance requiring additional consideration before an intervention. / expected reaction: Known response discussed in the context of a specific product. / vaccine hesitancy: Uncertainty or reluctance concerning vaccination. / informed choice: Decision made after relevant information and questions are addressed. / disease protection: Reduction in disease risk or consequences through an intervention. / breakthrough infection: Infection occurring despite relevant vaccination or protection. / catch-up schedule: Recommended sequence addressing previously missed vaccine doses. / administration record: Documentation of a product given and its relevant details. / safety monitoring: Ongoing observation and review of potential safety issues. / absolute risk: Probability or frequency of an outcome in a defined group and period. / qualified interpreter: Trained language professional supporting accurate communication between speakers. / teach-back: Checking an explanation by inviting the listener to restate its meaning. / patient preference: A person's stated wishes about care or communication. / clinical handoff: Transfer of relevant care information and responsibility between professionals. / documented plan: Agreed next steps recorded for the people involved. / source attribution: Identification of who reported, observed, or established a fact.

Rehearse with an AI colleague — complete prompt

Copy the full text below into your preferred AI. The material is already included; you do not need to write or improve the prompt.

TASK: Interactive workplace role-play
1. Use the supplied case or dialogue as the starting situation. Give a two-sentence briefing and offer two relevant professional roles for me to choose from. For a supplied dialogue, use its actual roles. Ask which role I want, then stop and wait. Do not write my replies.
2. After I choose, identify who you will play and the immediate communication goal. Play the other professional; where a meeting requires a third person, label each of your speakers clearly. Start with one natural workplace turn and wait for my reply. Keep most turns to one to three sentences and ask no more than one question at a time.
3. Let the exchange develop across six to ten learner turns, or end earlier if I type "feedback". Respond to what I actually say. Include a plausible clarification, disagreement or tradeoff without silently changing the starting facts. Mark any added constraint as a fictional second-round variation. Do not resolve approvals, evidence or commitments that remain uncertain.
4. Stay in role during the exchange. If my meaning is unclear, ask for clarification naturally. Give a hint only if I ask or cannot proceed. Do not deliver a model conversation in advance.
5. At the debrief, quote two of my phrases: one successful choice and one worth improving. Check factual accuracy, language, register and whether the next step was clear. Give up to three focused suggestions. Ask me to retry the weakest turn; wait. Only then offer an alternative wording and a harder replay.
Use voice replies when available. If I prefer selections, offer four plausible spoken replies and explain my choice before continuing. Do not require written compositions.

05Checking liquid-medicine instructionsThe label, the units, and the tool+

Your goal

Distinguish drug amount from liquid volume and check the actual instructions without calculating a dose.

This lesson in the book, page 40 ↗

A Read the situation

The label, the units, and the tool

Caregiver Omar sees mg on a prescription and mL on a measuring device and assumes they are interchangeable. Dr Cole explains the difference between drug amount, liquid volume, and concentration. The actual bottle, prescription, and appropriate measuring tool must be checked with the prescribing or dispensing team. No numerical dose, concentration, interval, or instruction to administer medicine is provided here.

Omar: Caregiver / Dr Cole: Pediatrician

Check the facts · 3 questions

Choose the answer supported by the briefing. Do not assume that a proposed action has already happened.

1. Which units does Omar confuse?

Answer and explanation

D. mg and mL

Omar treats milligrams and milliliters as interchangeable even though they measure different quantities.

2. What must be checked?

Answer and explanation

B. The actual bottle, prescription, and measuring tool

The case requires the actual verified product and instructions rather than a substitute.

3. What is deliberately not supplied?

Answer and explanation

D. A numerical dose

No dose or administration instruction is supplied for the learner to use clinically.

B Find the words

Vocabulary and collocations

Milligrams measure drug mass; milliliters measure liquid volume. The relationship depends on the actual concentration, which must not be guessed.

24 field terms and natural word combinations
milligram
Unit of mass used to express an amount of drug.distinguish milligrams
milliliter
Unit of liquid volume, abbreviated mL.measure in milliliters
concentration
Amount of drug contained in a stated volume or quantity.verify the concentration
dose amount
Quantity of drug prescribed for an administration.confirm the dose amount
dose volume
Volume of a liquid corresponding to a verified dose.verify the dose volume
oral syringe
Measuring device designed for administering liquid medicine by mouth.select the appropriate oral syringe
calibration mark
Mark indicating a measured quantity on a device.read the calibration marks
prescription label
Written instructions attached to a dispensed medicine.check the prescription label
active ingredient
Substance responsible for the medicine's intended pharmacological action.identify the active ingredient
duplicate ingredient
Same active substance present in more than one product.check for duplicate ingredients
administration interval
Time between prescribed administrations.confirm the administration interval
as-needed instruction
Direction describing use when specified conditions apply.clarify an as-needed instruction
maximum daily amount
Highest permitted amount over the specified daily period.verify the maximum daily amount
measuring device
Tool used to determine the required quantity.match the measuring device
dispensing pharmacy
Pharmacy supplying the prescribed product.contact the dispensing pharmacy
label discrepancy
Conflict between the label and another instruction or record.resolve a label discrepancy
caregiver demonstration
Caregiver showing how an instruction is understood.invite a caregiver demonstration
medication reconciliation
Process of verifying and aligning the actual medicine list.complete medication reconciliation
qualified interpreter
Trained language professional supporting accurate communication between speakers.request a qualified interpreter
teach-back
Checking an explanation by inviting the listener to restate its meaning.use teach-back without blame
patient preference
A person's stated wishes about care or communication.elicit the patient preference
clinical handoff
Transfer of relevant care information and responsibility between professionals.confirm the clinical handoff
documented plan
Agreed next steps recorded for the people involved.review the documented plan
source attribution
Identification of who reported, observed, or established a fact.preserve source attribution

Use the prescribed, verified instructions and appropriate device. Household spoons are not reliable measuring tools. This exercise does not authorize dose calculation or administration.

C Notice the language

Teach-back checks the explanation

Invite the caregiver to show how the verified instructions would be followed. Correct a misunderstanding without treating it as evidence of carelessness.

16 useful phrases, grouped by purpose
Separate the units
Milligrams and milliliters measure different things.
Name the missing link
The actual concentration connects amount and volume.
Use the real product
We need to check this bottle and its current label.
Avoid substitution
Do not assume another bottle has the same concentration.
Resolve a conflict
Let us clarify the conflicting instructions with the responsible team.
Check the tool
The measuring device needs to match the verified instructions.
Invite demonstration
Show me how you understand the verified instruction.
Own the explanation
Let me explain the units more clearly.
Invite correction
Please stop me if I have misunderstood.
Explain a term
In everyday language, that means the following.
Separate uncertainty
That remains uncertain; it is not a confirmed finding.
Check your explanation
Let me explain that another way.
Make space
We can pause before moving to the next point.
Name responsibility
Let us confirm who will follow up.
Keep a record accurate
I will distinguish what was reported from what was observed.
Confirm an agreement
We have agreed on the next step, not on an outcome.
6 language and precision notes
Amount versus volume
Keeps the quantity of drug distinct from the volume of liquid containing it.
This bottle
Anchors the check to the actual product rather than a remembered label.
Show me
Checks practical understanding of verified instructions, not mental arithmetic from an incomplete scenario.
Reported versus established
Attribute a person's account with reports or describes. Use confirms only when the relevant evidence supports confirmation.
Can versus will
Can describes an available possibility or ability; will makes a commitment. Do not turn a possible result into a promise.
Checking a message
A check-back confirms the meaning received. Correct the explanation calmly when the listener has understood something different.

D Check your understanding

Choose the precise message

Choose one best answer using the case facts and the stated purpose.

1. Which statement is correct?

Answer and explanation

A. mg measures mass; mL measures volume.

The units describe different quantities, linked by the actual product concentration.

2. What must not be guessed?

Answer and explanation

A. The concentration

A guessed concentration can make an amount-to-volume interpretation unsafe and is not supplied here.

3. Which tool is inappropriate for precise measurement?

Answer and explanation

D. An ordinary kitchen spoon

Household spoons vary and are not reliable substitutes for the appropriate medicine-measuring device.

4. What is the purpose of demonstration?

Answer and explanation

D. Check understanding of verified instructions

A demonstration checks how the explanation was understood using the real verified instructions.

Check explanations in the book, page 93 ↗

E Conversations

The label, the units, and the tool

Extended conversation · 20 turns · 10 gaps

Omar: Caregiver / Dr Cole: Pediatrician

Complete the numbered gaps. Use each bank entry once, without changing its form.

Word bank
  • concentration
  • oral syringe
  • dose volume
  • calibration mark
  • label discrepancy
  • dispensing pharmacy
  • milligram
  • caregiver demonstration
  • active ingredient
  • prescription label
  1. Omar
    The prescription says mg, but the measuring device says mL. I thought they meant the same amount.
  2. Dr Cole
    A 1 measures an amount of drug by mass. A milliliter measures liquid volume.
  3. Omar
    Then I cannot just copy the same number from one unit onto the other?
  4. Dr Cole
    Correct. The actual 2 links drug amount and liquid volume. We must not guess it.
  5. Omar
    I have an older bottle at home. Could I use its instructions if the name looks similar?
  6. Dr Cole
    We need this product's current 3 and verified instructions, not a remembered label from a different bottle.
  7. Omar
    I also need to know which measuring tool to use. A kitchen spoon seems easier.
  8. Dr Cole
    A kitchen spoon is not a reliable measure. The appropriate 4 or other specified device should match the verified instructions.
  9. Omar
    Some of the small lines on the device are difficult for me to distinguish.
  10. Dr Cole
    We should check the 5 spacing and whether the device is suitable, rather than asking you to estimate between unclear lines.
  11. Omar
    If the label and another instruction disagree, which one should I guess is current?
  12. Dr Cole
    Do not guess. A 6 needs clarification with the responsible prescribing or dispensing team.
  13. Omar
    Would the pharmacy be able to check the product it actually supplied?
  14. Dr Cole
    Yes, the 7 is an appropriate contact for verifying the dispensed product and its instructions.
  15. Omar
    I use another over-the-counter product sometimes. Should that be part of the review too?
  16. Dr Cole
    Yes. Reviewing the 8 helps identify possible duplication; similar or different brand names do not settle that question.
  17. Omar
    Once everything is verified, can I show you how I understand the instruction?
  18. Dr Cole
    A 9 is useful. It checks our explanation and the tool, not your intelligence.
  19. Omar
    I can now distinguish the amount of drug from the liquid that contains it.
  20. Dr Cole
    Exactly. The 10 must match the verified instructions. Let us check the bottle, prescription, and measuring device together before confirming what to give.

Answers and explanations
  1. milligram Turn 2. A milligram measures drug mass; a milliliter measures liquid volume.
  2. concentration Turn 4. Concentration links drug amount and volume and must be verified.
  3. prescription label Turn 6. The current prescription label cannot be replaced by another bottle's remembered instructions.
  4. oral syringe Turn 8. An oral syringe must suit the actual verified liquid-medicine instruction.
  5. calibration mark Turn 10. Calibration marks indicate measured quantities and must be readable and appropriate to the intended measurement.
  6. label discrepancy Turn 12. A label discrepancy is a conflict between instructions that needs clarification with the responsible prescribing or dispensing team.
  7. dispensing pharmacy Turn 14. The dispensing pharmacy supplied the product and is an appropriate contact for verifying its label, concentration, and measuring device.
  8. active ingredient Turn 16. The active ingredient is the pharmacologically active substance and should be checked across products rather than relying only on brand names.
  9. caregiver demonstration Turn 18. A caregiver demonstration shows how verified instructions are understood and allows the explanation or equipment to be corrected before use.
  10. dose volume Turn 20. Dose volume is the liquid quantity corresponding to a verified dose and concentration; it is not supplied or calculated in this exercise.
Read the complete conversation
  1. Omar: The prescription says mg, but the measuring device says mL. I thought they meant the same amount.
  2. Dr Cole: A milligram measures an amount of drug by mass. A milliliter measures liquid volume.
  3. Omar: Then I cannot just copy the same number from one unit onto the other?
  4. Dr Cole: Correct. The actual concentration links drug amount and liquid volume. We must not guess it.
  5. Omar: I have an older bottle at home. Could I use its instructions if the name looks similar?
  6. Dr Cole: We need this product's current prescription label and verified instructions, not a remembered label from a different bottle.
  7. Omar: I also need to know which measuring tool to use. A kitchen spoon seems easier.
  8. Dr Cole: A kitchen spoon is not a reliable measure. The appropriate oral syringe or other specified device should match the verified instructions.
  9. Omar: Some of the small lines on the device are difficult for me to distinguish.
  10. Dr Cole: We should check the calibration mark spacing and whether the device is suitable, rather than asking you to estimate between unclear lines.
  11. Omar: If the label and another instruction disagree, which one should I guess is current?
  12. Dr Cole: Do not guess. A label discrepancy needs clarification with the responsible prescribing or dispensing team.
  13. Omar: Would the pharmacy be able to check the product it actually supplied?
  14. Dr Cole: Yes, the dispensing pharmacy is an appropriate contact for verifying the dispensed product and its instructions.
  15. Omar: I use another over-the-counter product sometimes. Should that be part of the review too?
  16. Dr Cole: Yes. Reviewing the active ingredient helps identify possible duplication; similar or different brand names do not settle that question.
  17. Omar: Once everything is verified, can I show you how I understand the instruction?
  18. Dr Cole: A caregiver demonstration is useful. It checks our explanation and the tool, not your intelligence.
  19. Omar: I can now distinguish the amount of drug from the liquid that contains it.
  20. Dr Cole: Exactly. The dose volume must match the verified instructions. Let us check the bottle, prescription, and measuring device together before confirming what to give.
Dialogue in the book, page 46 ↗
Three more conversations at work

Each short conversation has its own fictional situation and facts.

The same brand name does not verify concentration

A caregiver compares two bottles.

  1. Caregiver: The new bottle has a familiar brand name. Can I assume the old volume instruction still applies?
  2. Pediatrician: We need the actual current product, concentration, and verified prescription instructions.
  3. Caregiver: I thought matching the name was enough to establish the same amount per milliliter.
  4. Pediatrician: That assumption can be unsafe; the real label must be checked.
  5. Caregiver: I will not convert the amount myself from a remembered instruction.
  6. Pediatrician: Any dose and volume need verification with the responsible prescribing or dispensing team.
  7. Caregiver: Could the pharmacist also check that the measuring device matches the instruction?
  8. Pediatrician: Yes. The actual product, label, and appropriate device belong together.
  9. Caregiver: Then this discussion is about resolving the information, not calculating a new dose.
  10. Pediatrician: Correct. No dose or administration instruction should be invented from this conversation.
Milligrams and milliliters name different quantities

A clinician checks the caregiver's understanding of units.

  1. Pediatrician: Please explain what you understand by milligrams and milliliters.
  2. Caregiver: Milligrams describe drug mass, while milliliters describe liquid volume.
  3. Pediatrician: Good. What connects the two for a particular liquid product?
  4. Caregiver: The actual concentration, which needs to be verified rather than guessed.
  5. Pediatrician: That distinction prevents the same number from being copied between different units.
  6. Caregiver: I also understand that an ordinary kitchen spoon is not a reliable measuring device.
  7. Pediatrician: We should use the appropriate device specified for the actual verified instruction.
  8. Caregiver: Could you check the markings with me after the label is confirmed?
  9. Pediatrician: Yes. A demonstration can check my explanation and the suitability of the tool.
  10. Caregiver: Then understanding the units does not authorize me to invent a dose from incomplete information.
Two caregivers need one verified instruction

A clinician addresses conflicting home medicine messages.

  1. Caregiver: One caregiver remembers a different instruction from the one on the current label.
  2. Pediatrician: We should resolve that conflict with the responsible team before treating either memory as definitive.
  3. Caregiver: I do not want the two caregivers to alternate between incompatible instructions.
  4. Pediatrician: Correct. A consistent plan needs the actual product and verified current direction.
  5. Caregiver: Could we document which instruction is superseded once the review is complete?
  6. Pediatrician: The approved record and communication process should make that clear.
  7. Caregiver: Both caregivers need the corrected explanation, not only the person who attended today.
  8. Pediatrician: With the appropriate permissions, we should confirm how the information reaches each responsible person.
  9. Caregiver: Then a resolved prescription question also needs a completed communication step.
  10. Pediatrician: Exactly. Internal agreement is not enough if conflicting instructions remain in use at home.

F Say it

Two scenarios to practice

The label, the units, and the tool
  1. Read the corrected exchange. Say milligrams and milliliters in full at first mention.
  2. Swap roles. Practice resolving the instruction conflict without inventing a dose or concentration.

On your own: read both roles. With a partner: swap roles after the first reading. Use the completed conversation to check any line you need to repeat.

Two products may share an ingredient

A caregiver brings two products with different brand names. Their active ingredients have not yet been checked. The clinician asks the responsible team to review them.

Choose one reply for each gap, then read the four turns in order. Check the explanation after each choice.

1. Caregiver: "The brand names are ___."

Answer and explanation

C. different

The products have different brand names in the supplied scenario.

2. Clinician: "The active ingredients remain ___."

Answer and explanation

B. unchecked

Different brands do not establish whether their active ingredients differ.

3. Caregiver: "We should not assume there is no ___."

Answer and explanation

D. duplication

The ingredients must be reviewed before excluding duplication.

4. Clinician: "The responsible team will ___ them."

Answer and explanation

C. review

The next step is the actual product review, not a guessed administration plan.

Read the completed exchange twice, switching roles. Keep the printed facts and professional responsibilities unchanged.

Lesson 05: Checking liquid-medicine instructions
Original fictional case: Caregiver Omar sees mg on a prescription and mL on a measuring device and assumes they are interchangeable. Dr Cole explains the difference between drug amount, liquid volume, and concentration. The actual bottle, prescription, and appropriate measuring tool must be checked with the prescribing or dispensing team. No numerical dose, concentration, interval, or instruction to administer medicine is provided here.
Language workshop: The label, the units, and the tool
Communication goal: Distinguish drug amount from liquid volume and check the actual instructions without calculating a dose.
Language guidance: Milligrams measure drug mass; milliliters measure liquid volume. The relationship depends on the actual concentration, which must not be guessed.
Useful expressions (preserve the supplied facts): Milligrams and milliliters measure different things. / The actual concentration connects amount and volume. / We need to check this bottle and its current label. / Do not assume another bottle has the same concentration. / Let us clarify the conflicting instructions with the responsible team. / The measuring device needs to match the verified instructions. / Show me how you understand the verified instruction. / Let me explain the units more clearly. / Please stop me if I have misunderstood. / In everyday language, that means the following. / That remains uncertain; it is not a confirmed finding. / Let me explain that another way. / We can pause before moving to the next point. / Let us confirm who will follow up. / I will distinguish what was reported from what was observed. / We have agreed on the next step, not on an outcome.
Vocabulary: milligram: Unit of mass used to express an amount of drug. / milliliter: Unit of liquid volume, abbreviated mL. / concentration: Amount of drug contained in a stated volume or quantity. / dose amount: Quantity of drug prescribed for an administration. / dose volume: Volume of a liquid corresponding to a verified dose. / oral syringe: Measuring device designed for administering liquid medicine by mouth. / calibration mark: Mark indicating a measured quantity on a device. / prescription label: Written instructions attached to a dispensed medicine. / active ingredient: Substance responsible for the medicine's intended pharmacological action. / duplicate ingredient: Same active substance present in more than one product. / administration interval: Time between prescribed administrations. / as-needed instruction: Direction describing use when specified conditions apply. / maximum daily amount: Highest permitted amount over the specified daily period. / measuring device: Tool used to determine the required quantity. / dispensing pharmacy: Pharmacy supplying the prescribed product. / label discrepancy: Conflict between the label and another instruction or record. / caregiver demonstration: Caregiver showing how an instruction is understood. / medication reconciliation: Process of verifying and aligning the actual medicine list. / qualified interpreter: Trained language professional supporting accurate communication between speakers. / teach-back: Checking an explanation by inviting the listener to restate its meaning. / patient preference: A person's stated wishes about care or communication. / clinical handoff: Transfer of relevant care information and responsibility between professionals. / documented plan: Agreed next steps recorded for the people involved. / source attribution: Identification of who reported, observed, or established a fact.

Rehearse with an AI colleague — complete prompt

Copy the full text below into your preferred AI. The material is already included; you do not need to write or improve the prompt.

TASK: Interactive workplace role-play
1. Use the supplied case or dialogue as the starting situation. Give a two-sentence briefing and offer two relevant professional roles for me to choose from. For a supplied dialogue, use its actual roles. Ask which role I want, then stop and wait. Do not write my replies.
2. After I choose, identify who you will play and the immediate communication goal. Play the other professional; where a meeting requires a third person, label each of your speakers clearly. Start with one natural workplace turn and wait for my reply. Keep most turns to one to three sentences and ask no more than one question at a time.
3. Let the exchange develop across six to ten learner turns, or end earlier if I type "feedback". Respond to what I actually say. Include a plausible clarification, disagreement or tradeoff without silently changing the starting facts. Mark any added constraint as a fictional second-round variation. Do not resolve approvals, evidence or commitments that remain uncertain.
4. Stay in role during the exchange. If my meaning is unclear, ask for clarification naturally. Give a hint only if I ask or cannot proceed. Do not deliver a model conversation in advance.
5. At the debrief, quote two of my phrases: one successful choice and one worth improving. Check factual accuracy, language, register and whether the next step was clear. Give up to three focused suggestions. Ask me to retry the weakest turn; wait. Only then offer an alternative wording and a harder replay.
Use voice replies when available. If I prefer selections, offer four plausible spoken replies and explain my choice before continuing. Do not require written compositions.

06Explaining adolescent confidentialityPrivacy without an impossible promise+

Your goal

Explain private discussion, record access, and safety limits before sensitive disclosure.

This lesson in the book, page 49 ↗

A Read the situation

Privacy without an impossible promise

Sixteen-year-old Alex asks Dr Hale whether everything discussed will remain private. Dr Hale explains that confidentiality, consent, caregiver access, portal settings, billing, and safety duties depend on the applicable rules and service. The clinician offers an appropriate private discussion and checks actual access arrangements. The case supplies no jurisdiction-specific legal conclusion.

Alex: Adolescent patient / Dr Hale: Pediatrician

Check the facts · 3 questions

Choose the answer supported by the briefing. Do not assume that a proposed action has already happened.

1. What does Alex ask?

Answer and explanation

B. Whether everything will remain private

Alex asks about the limits of privacy before sensitive discussion.

2. What does Dr Hale check?

Answer and explanation

A. Actual access arrangements and applicable rules

Privacy depends on the real service, records, and applicable requirements.

3. What is not supplied?

Answer and explanation

D. A jurisdiction-specific legal conclusion

The case does not determine the legal rules for a particular jurisdiction.

B Find the words

Vocabulary and collocations

Privacy in the room does not automatically make portal, billing, or later communications private. Check the actual channels.

24 field terms and natural word combinations
confidentiality
Duty to protect information within applicable rules and limits.explain confidentiality
private consultation
Discussion held without others present when appropriate.offer a private consultation
consent capacity
Ability to make the relevant informed decision under applicable standards.assess consent capacity
minor consent
Rules governing a minor's authorization for particular care.verify minor-consent rules
guardian authority
Legally recognized decision-making role for a child in context.clarify guardian authority
proxy access
Another person's permitted access to a patient's information system.check proxy access
portal visibility
Information viewable through a patient-access system.verify portal visibility
billing disclosure
Information potentially revealed through payment or insurance processes.explain possible billing disclosure
safety exception
Circumstance permitting or requiring disclosure for safety under applicable rules.explain a safety exception
mandatory reporting
Legally required communication of specified concerns to an authority.clarify mandatory-reporting duties
information sharing
Disclosure of relevant information to another person or service.discuss information sharing
minimum necessary information
Limited information appropriate to a particular authorized purpose.identify necessary information
preferred contact method
Communication route the person wishes the service to use.confirm the preferred contact method
confidential contact
Communication handled through an appropriately private route.arrange a confidential contact
record-access rule
Requirement governing who can see particular health information.verify record-access rules
privacy limitation
Boundary on what can be kept private in the actual setting.explain privacy limitations
safeguarding concern
Concern about safety or welfare requiring appropriate assessment or action.address a safeguarding concern
supported disclosure
Planned sharing of information with appropriate explanation and support.arrange supported disclosure
qualified interpreter
Trained language professional supporting accurate communication between speakers.request a qualified interpreter
teach-back
Checking an explanation by inviting the listener to restate its meaning.use teach-back without blame
patient preference
A person's stated wishes about care or communication.elicit the patient preference
clinical handoff
Transfer of relevant care information and responsibility between professionals.confirm the clinical handoff
documented plan
Agreed next steps recorded for the people involved.review the documented plan
source attribution
Identification of who reported, observed, or established a fact.preserve source attribution

Avoid universal claims about minor consent or caregiver access. Explain the applicable rules, safety obligations, and any limits before promising confidentiality.

C Notice the language

Explain limits before collecting the detail

Do not promise absolute secrecy and then reveal exceptions later. Make the actual privacy arrangements understandable without discouraging the adolescent from seeking care.

16 useful phrases, grouped by purpose
Avoid absolute secrecy
I cannot promise that every circumstance is completely private.
Explain before disclosure
Let us discuss the privacy limits before you share the detail.
Offer private time
We can arrange an appropriate conversation without your caregiver present.
Check access
I need to verify who can see this part of the record.
Discuss channels
Portal messages and billing may have different access arrangements.
Explain safety
I will explain the relevant safety duties in this setting.
Ask about contact
Which contact method is appropriate for us to use?
Support sharing
When sharing is needed, I will explain the process and support you.
Invite correction
Please stop me if I have misunderstood.
Explain a term
In everyday language, that means the following.
Separate uncertainty
That remains uncertain; it is not a confirmed finding.
Check your explanation
Let me explain that another way.
Make space
We can pause before moving to the next point.
Name responsibility
Let us confirm who will follow up.
Keep a record accurate
I will distinguish what was reported from what was observed.
Confirm an agreement
We have agreed on the next step, not on an outcome.
6 language and precision notes
Private versus confidential
Private describes the setting; confidentiality concerns handling information under applicable duties.
May have access
Preserves uncertainty until the actual access arrangements are checked.
Before you share
Places the explanation of limits before sensitive detail is requested.
Reported versus established
Attribute a person's account with reports or describes. Use confirms only when the relevant evidence supports confirmation.
Can versus will
Can describes an available possibility or ability; will makes a commitment. Do not turn a possible result into a promise.
Checking a message
A check-back confirms the meaning received. Correct the explanation calmly when the listener has understood something different.

D Check your understanding

Choose the precise message

Choose one best answer using the case facts and the stated purpose.

1. Which promise is unsafe?

Answer and explanation

B. Nothing you say can ever be shared under any circumstances.

Absolute secrecy ignores possible legal, safety, and record-access limits.

2. Which issue is separate from privacy in the room?

Answer and explanation

B. Portal and billing visibility

A private conversation can still generate records or communications with separate access arrangements.

3. Which legal statement is justified here?

Answer and explanation

C. Applicable rules must be verified.

The case supplies no jurisdiction and therefore requires checking the actual rules.

4. What should happen before sensitive disclosure?

Answer and explanation

B. Explain relevant privacy limits

Explaining limits first helps the adolescent understand how information may be handled.
