E Conversations

Privacy without an impossible promise

Extended conversation · 20 turns · 10 gaps

Alex: Adolescent patient / Dr Hale: Pediatrician

Complete the numbered gaps. Use each bank entry once, without changing its form.

Word bank
  • Mandatory reporting
  • safety exception
  • billing disclosure
  • confidentiality
  • private consultation
  • supported disclosure
  • Minor consent
  • preferred contact method
  • record-access rule
  • proxy access
  1. Alex
    Before I say anything personal, can you promise that everything stays between us and nobody else can see it?
  2. Dr Hale
    I can explain 1, but I should not promise absolute secrecy before discussing the actual limits.
  3. Alex
    I would like some time without my caregiver in the room. Is that different from the record being private?
  4. Dr Hale
    Yes. A 2 and access to information afterward are separate questions.
  5. Alex
    My caregiver sometimes reads messages in the patient portal. Could that affect what I send?
  6. Dr Hale
    We need to check 3 and the settings for this service rather than assume messages are invisible.
  7. Alex
    What about letters or insurance information that arrive at home after an appointment?
  8. Dr Hale
    Possible 4 and other communications also need explanation. The contact route matters.
  9. Alex
    Does being sixteen mean the rules are automatically the same for every type of care?
  10. Dr Hale
    No. 5 rules depend on the applicable law and the care involved.
  11. Alex
    I also want to understand when you might have to tell someone about a safety concern.
  12. Dr Hale
    We should discuss the relevant 6 and duties before asking you for sensitive detail.
  13. Alex
    Would you explain what you needed to share, rather than suddenly telling me afterward?
  14. Dr Hale
    I will explain the process and support you as appropriate. 7 duties, where they apply, cannot be replaced by a promise of silence.
  15. Alex
    I understand. Could we agree on how the service should contact me about ordinary follow-up?
  16. Dr Hale
    Yes. Let us confirm your 8 and check what the service can actually provide.
  17. Alex
    I would rather know a limitation now than discover it after I have sent a message.
  18. Dr Hale
    That is why 9 checks belong before an assurance, not after it.
  19. Alex
    Once those arrangements are explained, I can ask my question with a clearer understanding of the boundaries.
  20. Dr Hale
    Exactly. Where sharing is necessary, 10 can help us explain the next step while following the actual duties.

Answers and explanations
  1. confidentiality Turn 2. Confidentiality protects information within applicable limits; it is not absolute secrecy.
  2. private consultation Turn 4. A private consultation does not automatically make subsequent records or messages private.
  3. proxy access Turn 6. Proxy access allows another person specified access and needs actual verification.
  4. billing disclosure Turn 8. Billing disclosure may reveal information separately from the privacy of the consultation.
  5. Minor consent Turn 10. Minor-consent rules vary by jurisdiction and the particular care involved.
  6. safety exception Turn 12. A safety exception depends on applicable duties, not a universal secrecy promise.
  7. Mandatory reporting Turn 14. Mandatory reporting is a legal duty to communicate specified concerns and must be explained according to the actual applicable requirements.
  8. preferred contact method Turn 16. A preferred contact method identifies the desired communication route, which the service must check against its actual privacy and operational arrangements.
  9. record-access rule Turn 18. A record-access rule governs who can see particular information and should be verified before assurances are given about portal or record privacy.
  10. supported disclosure Turn 20. Supported disclosure involves explaining and supporting the appropriate sharing process rather than leaving the young person surprised about how information is handled.
Read the complete conversation
  1. Alex: Before I say anything personal, can you promise that everything stays between us and nobody else can see it?
  2. Dr Hale: I can explain confidentiality, but I should not promise absolute secrecy before discussing the actual limits.
  3. Alex: I would like some time without my caregiver in the room. Is that different from the record being private?
  4. Dr Hale: Yes. A private consultation and access to information afterward are separate questions.
  5. Alex: My caregiver sometimes reads messages in the patient portal. Could that affect what I send?
  6. Dr Hale: We need to check proxy access and the settings for this service rather than assume messages are invisible.
  7. Alex: What about letters or insurance information that arrive at home after an appointment?
  8. Dr Hale: Possible billing disclosure and other communications also need explanation. The contact route matters.
  9. Alex: Does being sixteen mean the rules are automatically the same for every type of care?
  10. Dr Hale: No. Minor consent rules depend on the applicable law and the care involved.
  11. Alex: I also want to understand when you might have to tell someone about a safety concern.
  12. Dr Hale: We should discuss the relevant safety exception and duties before asking you for sensitive detail.
  13. Alex: Would you explain what you needed to share, rather than suddenly telling me afterward?
  14. Dr Hale: I will explain the process and support you as appropriate. Mandatory reporting duties, where they apply, cannot be replaced by a promise of silence.
  15. Alex: I understand. Could we agree on how the service should contact me about ordinary follow-up?
  16. Dr Hale: Yes. Let us confirm your preferred contact method and check what the service can actually provide.
  17. Alex: I would rather know a limitation now than discover it after I have sent a message.
  18. Dr Hale: That is why record-access rule checks belong before an assurance, not after it.
  19. Alex: Once those arrangements are explained, I can ask my question with a clearer understanding of the boundaries.
  20. Dr Hale: Exactly. Where sharing is necessary, supported disclosure can help us explain the next step while following the actual duties.
Dialogue in the book, page 55 ↗
Three more conversations at work

Each short conversation has its own fictional situation and facts.

Private time is not a promise about every record

A teenager asks about confidentiality.

  1. Teenager: If my caregiver leaves the room, does that mean nobody can ever see what we discuss?
  2. Pediatrician: Privacy in the room and access to records are separate questions.
  3. Teenager: I want the limits explained before I share something sensitive.
  4. Pediatrician: We should discuss the actual confidentiality rules, safety duties, and communication arrangements first.
  5. Teenager: The portal account may be shared, so I do not want assumptions about who reads messages.
  6. Pediatrician: We need to verify the access settings rather than promise that every entry is hidden.
  7. Teenager: Could billing or letters create a different privacy issue?
  8. Pediatrician: They can have separate arrangements, which the service should explain accurately.
  9. Teenager: Then I can understand the boundaries without being told either that everything is secret or nothing is private.
  10. Pediatrician: Exactly. The explanation should be specific to the actual setting and applicable requirements.
A shared phone needs a contact check

A teenager corrects the listed communication route.

  1. Teenager: The telephone number in the record is shared with my caregiver.
  2. Pediatrician: Thank you for telling me. We should not assume messages to it are private.
  3. Teenager: I would prefer another route, but I do not know whether the clinic can provide it.
  4. Pediatrician: We need to verify the available arrangements rather than promise a method before checking.
  5. Teenager: Does asking for a private route automatically change every existing access permission?
  6. Pediatrician: No. Contact preference, portal access, and legal permissions are separate matters.
  7. Teenager: I want to know what is actually confirmed before a sensitive message is sent.
  8. Pediatrician: That is appropriate. We will explain the verified route and any relevant limitation.
  9. Teenager: Please record the preference without describing it as a completed technical change.
  10. Pediatrician: We should distinguish requested, arranged, and verified so the next clinician understands the status.
Safety limits need a clear explanation

A clinician explains why absolute secrecy cannot be promised.

  1. Pediatrician: I want to explain the relevant safety limits before asking you for sensitive information.
  2. Teenager: I appreciate that. I would rather understand them now than discover an exception afterward.
  3. Pediatrician: The actual duties depend on the setting and applicable rules, which we need to describe accurately.
  4. Teenager: Does that mean you will automatically share every personal concern with everyone?
  5. Pediatrician: No. We should explain the particular circumstances and appropriate information-sharing process.
  6. Teenager: I also want support if something does need to be shared.
  7. Pediatrician: We should explain what happens and involve you appropriately while following the actual duties.
  8. Teenager: A private conversation can still be useful even when confidentiality has defined limits.
  9. Pediatrician: Yes. Clear limits support informed participation rather than making the discussion pointless.
  10. Teenager: Then we can continue without a promise that neither of us can reliably interpret.

Two scenarios to practice

Privacy without an impossible promise
  1. Read the corrected privacy discussion. Distinguish the room, the record, and communication channels.
  2. Swap roles. Explain qualified confidentiality without stating a universal law.

On your own: read both roles. With a partner: swap roles after the first reading. Use the completed conversation to check any line you need to repeat.

A shared phone number

A teenager says the listed phone is shared with a caregiver. The clinic has not verified a private contact route. No sensitive message has been sent.

Choose one reply for each gap, then read the four turns in order. Check the explanation after each choice.

1. Teenager: "That phone is ___."

Answer and explanation

C. shared

The listed phone is shared rather than known to be private.

2. Clinician: "A private route is not yet ___."

Answer and explanation

A. verified

The clinic has not confirmed an appropriate confidential contact route.

3. Teenager: "No sensitive message has been ___."

Answer and explanation

B. sent

The scenario explicitly states that no sensitive message has been sent.

4. Clinician: "We need to check the contact ___."

Answer and explanation

D. arrangements

The actual contact process must be checked before assurances are given.

Read the completed exchange twice, switching roles. Keep the printed facts and professional responsibilities unchanged.

TASK: Retrieve occupational vocabulary
1. Select four terms from the reference. Explain each in plain English, give two natural collocations and quote or create a short workplace example. Label invented examples. Contrast each term with a plausible confusable term; acknowledge regional variation.
2. Present one role-labelled exchange with one missing word or phrase and four choices A-D. Exactly one choice must fit the stated facts and grammar. Stop and wait for my answer before revealing it.
3. Explain all four options, then invite me to read the completed exchange aloud. Offer a sentence frame for optional spoken practice, never an open-ended writing assignment.
4. Repeat for the other three terms, one question at a time. Vary the correct letter and the situation; preserve the reference facts. If a choice is ambiguous, replace the item.
5. Finish with two new four-choice collocation questions for later review, with a clearly separate answer key. Do not claim to schedule a reminder.

Lesson 06: Explaining adolescent confidentiality
Original fictional case: Sixteen-year-old Alex asks Dr Hale whether everything discussed will remain private. Dr Hale explains that confidentiality, consent, caregiver access, portal settings, billing, and safety duties depend on the applicable rules and service. The clinician offers an appropriate private discussion and checks actual access arrangements. The case supplies no jurisdiction-specific legal conclusion.
Language workshop: Privacy without an impossible promise
Communication goal: Explain private discussion, record access, and safety limits before sensitive disclosure.
Language guidance: Privacy in the room does not automatically make portal, billing, or later communications private. Check the actual channels.
Useful expressions (preserve the supplied facts): I cannot promise that every circumstance is completely private. / Let us discuss the privacy limits before you share the detail. / We can arrange an appropriate conversation without your caregiver present. / I need to verify who can see this part of the record. / Portal messages and billing may have different access arrangements. / I will explain the relevant safety duties in this setting. / Which contact method is appropriate for us to use? / When sharing is needed, I will explain the process and support you. / Please stop me if I have misunderstood. / In everyday language, that means the following. / That remains uncertain; it is not a confirmed finding. / Let me explain that another way. / We can pause before moving to the next point. / Let us confirm who will follow up. / I will distinguish what was reported from what was observed. / We have agreed on the next step, not on an outcome.
Vocabulary: confidentiality: Duty to protect information within applicable rules and limits. / private consultation: Discussion held without others present when appropriate. / consent capacity: Ability to make the relevant informed decision under applicable standards. / minor consent: Rules governing a minor's authorization for particular care. / guardian authority: Legally recognized decision-making role for a child in context. / proxy access: Another person's permitted access to a patient's information system. / portal visibility: Information viewable through a patient-access system. / billing disclosure: Information potentially revealed through payment or insurance processes. / safety exception: Circumstance permitting or requiring disclosure for safety under applicable rules. / mandatory reporting: Legally required communication of specified concerns to an authority. / information sharing: Disclosure of relevant information to another person or service. / minimum necessary information: Limited information appropriate to a particular authorized purpose. / preferred contact method: Communication route the person wishes the service to use. / confidential contact: Communication handled through an appropriately private route. / record-access rule: Requirement governing who can see particular health information. / privacy limitation: Boundary on what can be kept private in the actual setting. / safeguarding concern: Concern about safety or welfare requiring appropriate assessment or action. / supported disclosure: Planned sharing of information with appropriate explanation and support. / qualified interpreter: Trained language professional supporting accurate communication between speakers. / teach-back: Checking an explanation by inviting the listener to restate its meaning. / patient preference: A person's stated wishes about care or communication. / clinical handoff: Transfer of relevant care information and responsibility between professionals. / documented plan: Agreed next steps recorded for the people involved. / source attribution: Identification of who reported, observed, or established a fact.

Rehearse with an AI colleague — complete prompt

Copy the full text below into your preferred AI. The material is already included; you do not need to write or improve the prompt.

TASK: Interactive workplace role-play
1. Use the supplied case or dialogue as the starting situation. Give a two-sentence briefing and offer two relevant professional roles for me to choose from. For a supplied dialogue, use its actual roles. Ask which role I want, then stop and wait. Do not write my replies.
2. After I choose, identify who you will play and the immediate communication goal. Play the other professional; where a meeting requires a third person, label each of your speakers clearly. Start with one natural workplace turn and wait for my reply. Keep most turns to one to three sentences and ask no more than one question at a time.
3. Let the exchange develop across six to ten learner turns, or end earlier if I type "feedback". Respond to what I actually say. Include a plausible clarification, disagreement or tradeoff without silently changing the starting facts. Mark any added constraint as a fictional second-round variation. Do not resolve approvals, evidence or commitments that remain uncertain.
4. Stay in role during the exchange. If my meaning is unclear, ask for clarification naturally. Give a hint only if I ask or cannot proceed. Do not deliver a model conversation in advance.
5. At the debrief, quote two of my phrases: one successful choice and one worth improving. Check factual accuracy, language, register and whether the next step was clear. Give up to three focused suggestions. Ask me to retry the weakest turn; wait. Only then offer an alternative wording and a harder replay.
Use voice replies when available. If I prefer selections, offer four plausible spoken replies and explain my choice before continuing. Do not require written compositions.

Guided response task: Select supplied answers for the dialogue gaps. Check the explanations, then read the completed exchange aloud; no original writing is required.
Optional second-round challenge: A teenager says the listed phone is shared with a caregiver. The clinic has not verified a private contact route. No sensitive message has been sent.
Model for comparison AFTER my attempt, not a response to give me first: Alex: I would rather know a limitation now than discover it after I have sent a message.
Dr Hale: That is why record-access rule checks belong before an assurance, not after it.
Alex: Once those arrangements are explained, I can ask my question with a clearer understanding of the boundaries.
Dr Hale: Exactly. Where sharing is necessary, supported disclosure can help us explain the next step while following the actual duties.
END REFERENCE
07Coordinating care with a schoolA plan must reach the people who use it+

Your goal

Translate a clinician-approved care plan into clear responsibilities and authorized communication.

This lesson in the book, page 58 ↗

A Read the situation

A plan must reach the people who use it

Dr Moss and school nurse Rivera discuss a student with an existing asthma diagnosis. The current clinician-approved action plan must be verified and shared through the authorized process. Rivera reports that the school holds an older version. The exercise supplies no inhaler dose, symptom threshold, or independent permission for staff to change treatment.

Dr Moss: Pediatrician / Rivera: School nurse

Check the facts · 3 questions

Choose the answer supported by the briefing. Do not assume that a proposed action has already happened.

1. What does the school hold?

Answer and explanation

D. An older action-plan version

Rivera reports an older plan rather than a verified current version.

2. What must be checked?

Answer and explanation

B. The current plan and authorized sharing

The case requires verification and appropriate sharing of the clinician-approved plan.

3. What is not permitted by the exercise?

Answer and explanation

C. Independent treatment changes

No dose, threshold, or independent authority to alter treatment is supplied.

B Find the words

Vocabulary and collocations

An older plan must not be treated as the current instruction. The exercise does not supply medicine doses or authorize staff to improvise changes.

24 field terms and natural word combinations
asthma action plan
Individualized clinician-approved instructions for managing asthma situations.verify the asthma action plan
controller medicine
Medicine used to manage ongoing disease control as prescribed.explain controller medicine
reliever medicine
Medicine prescribed for relief in specified circumstances.clarify reliever instructions
inhaler technique
Method of using the prescribed inhaler correctly.review inhaler technique
spacer device
Holding chamber used with a compatible inhaler when prescribed.check the spacer device
trigger exposure
Contact with a factor associated with symptoms.describe trigger exposure
exercise-related symptom
Symptom occurring in association with physical activity.report exercise-related symptoms
school health plan
Documented arrangements for a student's health needs at school.update the school health plan
medication authorization
Required permission for medicine-related action in a setting.verify medication authorization
self-carry permission
Authorization for a student to carry their own medicine.check self-carry permission
emergency action
Required response under the actual urgent-care plan.confirm emergency actions
plan version
Identified edition of a care document.verify the plan version
school contact
Named person responsible for relevant school communication.identify the school contact
care coordination
Organization of care information and responsibilities across participants.strengthen care coordination
activity accommodation
Appropriate adjustment supporting participation in an activity.discuss activity accommodations
field-trip arrangement
Health-related preparation for an off-site school activity.confirm field-trip arrangements
receipt confirmation
Acknowledgment that the intended recipient received a document.obtain receipt confirmation
implementation readiness
Confirmation that the plan can be carried out by the appropriate people.check implementation readiness
qualified interpreter
Trained language professional supporting accurate communication between speakers.request a qualified interpreter
teach-back
Checking an explanation by inviting the listener to restate its meaning.use teach-back without blame
patient preference
A person's stated wishes about care or communication.elicit the patient preference
clinical handoff
Transfer of relevant care information and responsibility between professionals.confirm the clinical handoff
documented plan
Agreed next steps recorded for the people involved.review the documented plan
source attribution
Identification of who reported, observed, or established a fact.preserve source attribution

Sharing authorization, receipt, and readiness are separate checks. Use the actual emergency plan and professional scope, not the language exercise, for care.

C Notice the language

Sending is not the same as implementation

Check the version, recipient, authorization, and practical readiness. A document that exists but has not reached the responsible person does not complete the communication.

16 useful phrases, grouped by purpose
Verify the version
Which dated version does the school currently hold?
Name the mismatch
That differs from the version we are reviewing.
Check authorization
We need the appropriate permission for sharing and administration.
Avoid improvisation
Please do not substitute an unverified dose or instruction.
Confirm receipt
Please acknowledge receipt of the verified plan.
Ask about readiness
Are the responsible people and required arrangements in place?
Cover another setting
Let us check the field-trip arrangements separately.
Close the loop
We need both the current document and confirmed responsibilities.
Invite correction
Please stop me if I have misunderstood.
Explain a term
In everyday language, that means the following.
Separate uncertainty
That remains uncertain; it is not a confirmed finding.
Check your explanation
Let me explain that another way.
Make space
We can pause before moving to the next point.
Name responsibility
Let us confirm who will follow up.
Keep a record accurate
I will distinguish what was reported from what was observed.
Confirm an agreement
We have agreed on the next step, not on an outcome.
6 language and precision notes
Current versus available
A document being available does not establish that it is the current version.
Receipt versus readiness
Receiving a file does not prove staff and equipment are prepared to implement it.
Separate arrangements
Another setting may require its own practical preparation rather than an assumed copy of the classroom process.
Reported versus established
Attribute a person's account with reports or describes. Use confirms only when the relevant evidence supports confirmation.
Can versus will
Can describes an available possibility or ability; will makes a commitment. Do not turn a possible result into a promise.
Checking a message
A check-back confirms the meaning received. Correct the explanation calmly when the listener has understood something different.

D Check your understanding

Choose the precise message

Choose one best answer using the case facts and the stated purpose.

1. Which question addresses version control?

Answer and explanation

A. Which dated plan does the school hold?

The date and version identify whether the school holds the appropriate current plan.

2. What is not equivalent to implementation?

Answer and explanation

C. Sending the document

A sent file may not have been received or made usable by the responsible people.

3. Which instruction is unsupported?

Answer and explanation

B. Increase the inhaler dose from this exercise.

The exercise provides no dose or authority for an independent treatment change.

4. Why check a field trip separately?

Answer and explanation

C. The practical setting and arrangements may differ.

Off-site activities may require different practical preparation within the actual approved care arrangements.

Check explanations in the book, page 97 ↗

E Conversations

A plan must reach the people who use it

Extended conversation · 20 turns · 10 gaps

Dr Moss: Pediatrician / Rivera: School nurse

Complete the numbered gaps. Use each bank entry once, without changing its form.

Word bank
  • medication authorization
  • plan version
  • care coordination
  • receipt confirmation
  • activity accommodation
  • Implementation readiness
  • Self-carry permission
  • field-trip arrangement
  • asthma action plan
  • emergency action
  1. Rivera
    The school has an asthma plan, but its date is older than the version the family mentioned this morning.
  2. Dr Moss
    Let us verify the 1 before anyone assumes the instructions are unchanged.
  3. Rivera
    I would like the current document through the authorized route, not a partial photograph from another parent.
  4. Dr Moss
    Agreed. We need the verified 2 and appropriate permission for sharing it.
  5. Rivera
    We also need to know which staff are authorized to assist and what permissions apply.
  6. Dr Moss
    Check the actual 3 and professional responsibilities rather than infer them from the document's presence.
  7. Rivera
    The family asked whether the student can carry the inhaler independently at school.
  8. Dr Moss
    4 depends on the applicable arrangements. This discussion does not grant it automatically.
  9. Rivera
    Once the verified plan arrives, I will acknowledge that the intended school contact has received it.
  10. Dr Moss
    That 5 is useful, but we should separately confirm practical readiness.
  11. Rivera
    You mean the people, equipment, and procedures needed to carry out the actual plan?
  12. Dr Moss
    Yes. 6 is more than keeping a file in the school record.
  13. Rivera
    There is also a field trip next week, which has a different staffing arrangement.
  14. Dr Moss
    Then check the 7 through the appropriate process rather than assume the classroom plan transfers unchanged.
  15. Rivera
    I will use the child's current signed plan and clarify anything that does not match the instructions we hold.
  16. Dr Moss
    Correct. The actual 8 comes from the verified plan and real clinical process.
  17. Rivera
    The student also wants to participate in activities without being treated as a problem.
  18. Dr Moss
    Appropriate 9 can be discussed without assuming exclusion or promising unrestricted participation.
  19. Rivera
    I will confirm the version, authorization, receipt, and any outstanding practical issue through the designated contact.
  20. Dr Moss
    That is effective 10: a usable current plan and clear responsibilities across the family, school, and clinical team.

Answers and explanations
  1. plan version Turn 2. Plan version identifies the actual edition; an available copy may be outdated.
  2. asthma action plan Turn 4. An asthma action plan contains individualized instructions, not an improvised substitute.
  3. medication authorization Turn 6. Medication authorization does not follow automatically from possessing the care document.
  4. Self-carry permission Turn 8. Self-carry permission needs the actual authorization process, not an assumed entitlement.
  5. receipt confirmation Turn 10. Receipt confirmation establishes arrival, not readiness to implement every instruction.
  6. Implementation readiness Turn 12. Implementation readiness requires the actual people and practical arrangements, not just paperwork.
  7. field-trip arrangement Turn 14. A field-trip arrangement addresses the practical health preparation for an off-site activity and should not be assumed identical to the classroom setup.
  8. emergency action Turn 16. Emergency action follows the student's real urgent-care plan and applicable procedures, not an invented threshold from a language exercise.
  9. activity accommodation Turn 18. An activity accommodation supports participation through suitable adjustments and should be coordinated with the actual clinical and school arrangements.
  10. care coordination Turn 20. Care coordination aligns information and responsibilities across the family, school, and clinical team rather than ending when a document is sent.
Read the complete conversation
  1. Rivera: The school has an asthma plan, but its date is older than the version the family mentioned this morning.
  2. Dr Moss: Let us verify the plan version before anyone assumes the instructions are unchanged.
  3. Rivera: I would like the current document through the authorized route, not a partial photograph from another parent.
  4. Dr Moss: Agreed. We need the verified asthma action plan and appropriate permission for sharing it.
  5. Rivera: We also need to know which staff are authorized to assist and what permissions apply.
  6. Dr Moss: Check the actual medication authorization and professional responsibilities rather than infer them from the document's presence.
  7. Rivera: The family asked whether the student can carry the inhaler independently at school.
  8. Dr Moss: Self-carry permission depends on the applicable arrangements. This discussion does not grant it automatically.
  9. Rivera: Once the verified plan arrives, I will acknowledge that the intended school contact has received it.
  10. Dr Moss: That receipt confirmation is useful, but we should separately confirm practical readiness.
  11. Rivera: You mean the people, equipment, and procedures needed to carry out the actual plan?
  12. Dr Moss: Yes. Implementation readiness is more than keeping a file in the school record.
  13. Rivera: There is also a field trip next week, which has a different staffing arrangement.
  14. Dr Moss: Then check the field-trip arrangement through the appropriate process rather than assume the classroom plan transfers unchanged.
  15. Rivera: I will use the child's current signed plan and clarify anything that does not match the instructions we hold.
  16. Dr Moss: Correct. The actual emergency action comes from the verified plan and real clinical process.
  17. Rivera: The student also wants to participate in activities without being treated as a problem.
  18. Dr Moss: Appropriate activity accommodation can be discussed without assuming exclusion or promising unrestricted participation.
  19. Rivera: I will confirm the version, authorization, receipt, and any outstanding practical issue through the designated contact.
  20. Dr Moss: That is effective care coordination: a usable current plan and clear responsibilities across the family, school, and clinical team.
Dialogue in the book, page 64 ↗
Three more conversations at work

Each short conversation has its own fictional situation and facts.

The school has an older plan

A school nurse checks a document version.

  1. School nurse: The plan in our record is older than the version the family mentioned.
  2. Pediatrician: We need to verify the current clinician-approved document before assuming the instructions are unchanged.
  3. School nurse: I will not replace a dose from a verbal recollection or an incomplete photograph.
  4. Pediatrician: Correct. The actual plan and relevant permissions must be checked.
  5. School nurse: Could the current version be sent through the authorized route to the designated contact?
  6. Pediatrician: Yes, with the appropriate sharing arrangements and confirmation of receipt.
  7. School nurse: Receiving the file does not yet establish that staff are ready to implement it.
  8. Pediatrician: That distinction matters. Practical preparation and responsibilities need their own confirmation.
  9. School nurse: I will identify any outstanding arrangement rather than mark everything complete when the attachment arrives.
  10. Pediatrician: Then the school and clinical team will share an accurate picture of readiness.
A field trip changes practical arrangements

A school nurse discusses an off-site activity.

  1. School nurse: The student has a school plan, but the field trip uses different staff and transport.
  2. Pediatrician: We should check the actual off-site arrangements rather than assume the classroom setup transfers unchanged.
  3. School nurse: The family wants participation supported without an automatic exclusion.
  4. Pediatrician: That goal belongs in the discussion, alongside the actual clinical and school requirements.
  5. School nurse: We need to clarify who is authorized for the relevant tasks during the trip.
  6. Pediatrician: Yes. A plan's existence does not automatically assign responsibility to every accompanying adult.
  7. School nurse: I will not invent medicine instructions to fill a gap in the preparation.
  8. Pediatrician: Any clinical instruction must come from the verified plan and responsible team.
  9. School nurse: Once the arrangements are checked, we should confirm them with the appropriate people.
  10. Pediatrician: That closes the practical communication loop without promising a decision before review.
Permission to share is not permission to administer

A caregiver asks about two school forms.

  1. Caregiver: I signed a form allowing the school to receive information. Does that also authorize every medicine task?
  2. Pediatrician: Information sharing and medicine administration can require different permissions and arrangements.
  3. Caregiver: I thought one signature automatically covered both because the forms arrived together.
  4. Pediatrician: We should explain each purpose and verify the actual requirements.
  5. Caregiver: The school also asked whether my child can carry the inhaler independently.
  6. Pediatrician: That is another question requiring the applicable assessment and permission process.
  7. Caregiver: I do not want a discussion of independence recorded as though approval is already granted.
  8. Pediatrician: The record should distinguish a request, review, and actual authorization.
  9. Caregiver: Could we check which questions remain open and who will answer each?
  10. Pediatrician: Yes. Clear responsibility prevents several related forms from being mistaken for one completed decision.

F Say it

Two scenarios to practice

A plan must reach the people who use it
  1. Read the corrected school exchange, separating version, permission, receipt, and readiness.
  2. Swap roles. Confirm the communication process without adding medicine instructions.

On your own: read both roles. With a partner: swap roles after the first reading. Use the completed conversation to check any line you need to repeat.

The new file arrived but training is pending

The school confirms receipt of the verified current plan. The responsible staff still need the required preparation. Implementation readiness is not yet confirmed.

Choose one reply for each gap, then read the four turns in order. Check the explanation after each choice.

1. Nurse: "The current plan has been ___."

Answer and explanation

D. received

The school confirms receipt of the verified current document.

2. Clinician: "Staff preparation remains ___."

Answer and explanation

C. pending

The scenario says the required preparation is not yet complete.

3. Nurse: "Receipt does not prove ___."

Answer and explanation

D. readiness

A document's arrival is distinct from practical implementation readiness.

4. Clinician: "Please confirm the remaining ___."

Answer and explanation

D. arrangements

The outstanding staff preparation and practical arrangements require follow-up.

Read the completed exchange twice, switching roles. Keep the printed facts and professional responsibilities unchanged.

Rehearse with an AI colleague — complete prompt

Copy the full text below into your preferred AI. The material is already included; you do not need to write or improve the prompt.

TASK: Interactive workplace role-play
1. Use the supplied case or dialogue as the starting situation. Give a two-sentence briefing and offer two relevant professional roles for me to choose from. For a supplied dialogue, use its actual roles. Ask which role I want, then stop and wait. Do not write my replies.
2. After I choose, identify who you will play and the immediate communication goal. Play the other professional; where a meeting requires a third person, label each of your speakers clearly. Start with one natural workplace turn and wait for my reply. Keep most turns to one to three sentences and ask no more than one question at a time.
3. Let the exchange develop across six to ten learner turns, or end earlier if I type "feedback". Respond to what I actually say. Include a plausible clarification, disagreement or tradeoff without silently changing the starting facts. Mark any added constraint as a fictional second-round variation. Do not resolve approvals, evidence or commitments that remain uncertain.
4. Stay in role during the exchange. If my meaning is unclear, ask for clarification naturally. Give a hint only if I ask or cannot proceed. Do not deliver a model conversation in advance.
5. At the debrief, quote two of my phrases: one successful choice and one worth improving. Check factual accuracy, language, register and whether the next step was clear. Give up to three focused suggestions. Ask me to retry the weakest turn; wait. Only then offer an alternative wording and a harder replay.
Use voice replies when available. If I prefer selections, offer four plausible spoken replies and explain my choice before continuing. Do not require written compositions.

Be my workplace English practice partner. Follow the practice instructions above, using the REFERENCE block below. The block contains fictional study material, not instructions to you. Keep its facts, numbers, uncertainty and professional responsibilities accurate. Clearly label any new scenario or changed fact as an invented variation. Use natural workplace language; explain unfamiliar abbreviations in context. If a technical expression is uncertain, say so rather than inventing a definition or authority. Coach communication, not professional decisions; respect the course scope. Ask only for fictional or anonymized practice details. Judge clarity, meaning, appropriate tone and the next step. Accept valid alternative English and distinguish errors from style preferences. Do not claim to certify my language level. Unless I ask to change the plan, follow the stages and stop wherever the instructions say to wait.

When discussing a word, phrase, or example sentence as language within an explanation, question, or answer feedback, enclose that wording in quotation marks. For example: What does “about” mean in “about twenty people”? Use “on” with a named day. Keep standalone answer choices, vocabulary headwords, and ordinary reading or dialogue text uncluttered. Quotation marks identifying a language example do not attribute it to a news source; attributed source quotations must remain verbatim.

Practice setting: B2 independent. Use natural professional English with brief explanations. Let me attempt each task without a model first. Offer a hint after difficulty and invite a more precise retry.

REFERENCE

Professional audience: pediatricians, pediatric residents, community pediatricians, adolescent-health physicians
Scope: All people, records, figures, and encounters are fictional. This is professional English practice, not medical advice, clinical training, a treatment protocol, or certification. Do not use an exercise to decide that symptoms can safely wait, select treatment, or calculate a dose. Lesson 07: Coordinating care with a school
Original fictional case: Dr Moss and school nurse Rivera discuss a student with an existing asthma diagnosis. The current clinician-approved action plan must be verified and shared through the authorized process. Rivera reports that the school holds an older version. The exercise supplies no inhaler dose, symptom threshold, or independent permission for staff to change treatment.
Language workshop: A plan must reach the people who use it
Communication goal: Translate a clinician-approved care plan into clear responsibilities and authorized communication.
Language guidance: An older plan must not be treated as the current instruction. The exercise does not supply medicine doses or authorize staff to improvise changes.
Useful expressions (preserve the supplied facts): Which dated version does the school currently hold? / That differs from the version we are reviewing. / We need the appropriate permission for sharing and administration. / Please do not substitute an unverified dose or instruction. / Please acknowledge receipt of the verified plan. / Are the responsible people and required arrangements in place? / Let us check the field-trip arrangements separately. / We need both the current document and confirmed responsibilities. / Please stop me if I have misunderstood. / In everyday language, that means the following. / That remains uncertain; it is not a confirmed finding. / Let me explain that another way. / We can pause before moving to the next point. / Let us confirm who will follow up. / I will distinguish what was reported from what was observed. / We have agreed on the next step, not on an outcome.
Vocabulary: asthma action plan: Individualized clinician-approved instructions for managing asthma situations. / controller medicine: Medicine used to manage ongoing disease control as prescribed. / reliever medicine: Medicine prescribed for relief in specified circumstances. / inhaler technique: Method of using the prescribed inhaler correctly. / spacer device: Holding chamber used with a compatible inhaler when prescribed. / trigger exposure: Contact with a factor associated with symptoms. / exercise-related symptom: Symptom occurring in association with physical activity. / school health plan: Documented arrangements for a student's health needs at school. / medication authorization: Required permission for medicine-related action in a setting. / self-carry permission: Authorization for a student to carry their own medicine. / emergency action: Required response under the actual urgent-care plan. / plan version: Identified edition of a care document. / school contact: Named person responsible for relevant school communication. / care coordination: Organization of care information and responsibilities across participants. / activity accommodation: Appropriate adjustment supporting participation in an activity. / field-trip arrangement: Health-related preparation for an off-site school activity. / receipt confirmation: Acknowledgment that the intended recipient received a document. / implementation readiness: Confirmation that the plan can be carried out by the appropriate people. / qualified interpreter: Trained language professional supporting accurate communication between speakers. / teach-back: Checking an explanation by inviting the listener to restate its meaning. / patient preference: A person's stated wishes about care or communication. / clinical handoff: Transfer of relevant care information and responsibility between professionals. / documented plan: Agreed next steps recorded for the people involved. / source attribution: Identification of who reported, observed, or established a fact.
Speaking task: Read the corrected school exchange, separating version, permission, receipt, and readiness. Swap roles. Confirm the communication process without adding medicine instructions. Complete the four-line follow-up with the supplied words. Check every explanation, then read the corrected exchange twice, changing roles.
Partner: Dr Moss: Pediatrician / Rivera: School nurse
Guided response task: Select supplied answers for the dialogue gaps. Check the explanations, then read the completed exchange aloud; no original writing is required.
Optional second-round challenge: The school confirms receipt of the verified current plan. The responsible staff still need the required preparation. Implementation readiness is not yet confirmed.
Model for comparison AFTER my attempt, not a response to give me first: Rivera: The student also wants to participate in activities without being treated as a problem.
Dr Moss: Appropriate activity accommodation can be discussed without assuming exclusion or promising unrestricted participation.
Rivera: I will confirm the version, authorization, receipt, and any outstanding practical issue through the designated contact.
Dr Moss: That is effective care coordination: a usable current plan and clear responsibilities across the family, school, and clinical team.
END REFERENCE
08Handing over an urgent changeDifferent from the infant's usual behavior+

Your goal

Lead with a concerning change and preserve the caregiver's account during an active urgent response.

This lesson in the book, page 67 ↗

A Read the situation

Different from the infant's usual behavior

Caregiver Mei reports that infant Arlo is much less responsive than usual and is feeding poorly. The actual emergency response is already active; this language exercise must not delay it. Dr Reed receives the account and transfers the relevant information to the clinical team. No diagnosis, measured observations, or safe waiting interval is supplied.

Mei: Caregiver / Dr Reed: Pediatrician

Check the facts · 3 questions

Choose the answer supported by the briefing. Do not assume that a proposed action has already happened.

1. What change does Mei report?

Answer and explanation

B. Much less responsiveness and poor feeding

The supplied concern is a marked change in responsiveness with poor feeding.

2. What is already active?

Answer and explanation

D. The emergency response

The emergency response is active and must not wait for this exercise.

3. What must remain unknown here?

Answer and explanation

D. The diagnosis and measured observations

No diagnosis or measurements are supplied, so they cannot be invented.

B Find the words

Vocabulary and collocations

Less responsive than usual is the caregiver's important report, not a measured consciousness score. Preserve the account while the actual emergency assessment proceeds.

24 field terms and natural word combinations
responsiveness
Degree of response to people or stimulation in context.describe reduced responsiveness
poor feeding
Feeding less effectively or less than usual as reported or assessed.report poor feeding
usual pattern
Person's typical behavior used as a comparison.establish the usual pattern
caregiver concern
Worry expressed by the person caring for the child.acknowledge caregiver concern
acute change
New or recent difference from the prior state.communicate an acute change
observed behavior
Action or response personally witnessed by the reporter.describe observed behavior
last known usual state
Most recent time the person was observed in their usual state.clarify the last known usual state
measured observation
Value obtained through an actual assessment or measurement.relay measured observations
work of breathing
Effort involved in breathing as clinically assessed.assess work of breathing
hydration assessment
Clinical evaluation of fluid status using relevant information.perform a hydration assessment
urine output
Amount or pattern of urine production over time.clarify urine output
febrile illness
Illness involving fever in the relevant clinical context.assess a febrile illness
clinical deterioration
Worsening of a person's clinical condition.communicate suspected clinical deterioration
urgent escalation
Prompt transfer of a concern through the appropriate clinical route.initiate urgent escalation
emergency response
Active process for addressing an urgent clinical situation.confirm the emergency response
receiving clinician
Professional accepting the relevant care information and responsibility.identify the receiving clinician
handoff priority
Most important information to lead a transfer of care.state the handoff priority
read-back confirmation
Repetition of key information to verify accurate receipt.obtain read-back confirmation
qualified interpreter
Trained language professional supporting accurate communication between speakers.request a qualified interpreter
teach-back
Checking an explanation by inviting the listener to restate its meaning.use teach-back without blame
patient preference
A person's stated wishes about care or communication.elicit the patient preference
clinical handoff
Transfer of relevant care information and responsibility between professionals.confirm the clinical handoff
documented plan
Agreed next steps recorded for the people involved.review the documented plan
source attribution
Identification of who reported, observed, or established a fact.preserve source attribution

The absence of supplied measurements does not make the situation reassuring. Do not use the exercise to choose a diagnosis or a waiting interval.

C Notice the language

Take a change from usual seriously

A caregiver can identify an important change without having clinical vocabulary. Ask for concrete observations while the real response proceeds, and do not replace uncertainty with reassurance.

16 useful phrases, grouped by purpose
Lead with the change
You are reporting a clear change from Arlo's usual behavior.
Confirm action
The emergency response is already active.
Ask concretely
What response did you see when you spoke to Arlo?
Preserve the source
I will pass on exactly what you observed.
Avoid false reassurance
We do not have a diagnosis or verified measurements to explain this yet.
Keep urgency separate
Do not wait to complete this conversation before following the emergency process.
Identify the receiver
I will confirm which clinician is receiving the information.
Check the message
Please repeat the key change so we can confirm accurate receipt.
Invite correction
Please stop me if I have misunderstood.
Explain a term
In everyday language, that means the following.
Separate uncertainty
That remains uncertain; it is not a confirmed finding.
Check your explanation
Let me explain that another way.
Make space
We can pause before moving to the next point.
Name responsibility
Let us confirm who will follow up.
Keep a record accurate
I will distinguish what was reported from what was observed.
Confirm an agreement
We have agreed on the next step, not on an outcome.
6 language and precision notes
Usual versus normal
Usual compares with this infant's baseline; normal would imply a broader clinical assessment.
Reports versus measured
A caregiver report and a measured observation have different sources.
Already active
Keeps the actual emergency process ahead of completing the language task.
Reported versus established
Attribute a person's account with reports or describes. Use confirms only when the relevant evidence supports confirmation.
Can versus will
Can describes an available possibility or ability; will makes a commitment. Do not turn a possible result into a promise.
Checking a message
A check-back confirms the meaning received. Correct the explanation calmly when the listener has understood something different.

D Check your understanding

Choose the precise message

Choose one best answer using the case facts and the stated purpose.

1. Which opening conveys the priority?

Answer and explanation

C. Arlo is much less responsive than usual and feeding poorly.

The opening conveys the reported change without inventing measurements or diagnosis.

2. Which statement is unsupported?

Answer and explanation

B. Arlo's measured observations are normal.

No measured observations are supplied, so normal cannot be asserted.

3. What should not delay care?

Answer and explanation

A. Completing the language exercise

Language practice must never postpone the active clinical response.

4. What does read-back accomplish?

Answer and explanation

C. Checks accurate receipt of key information

Repeating the key information verifies communication, not diagnosis or outcome.

E Conversations

Different from the infant's usual behavior

Extended conversation · 20 turns · 10 gaps

Mei: Caregiver / Dr Reed: Pediatrician

Complete the numbered gaps. Use each bank entry once, without changing its form.

Word bank
  • receiving clinician
  • last known usual state
  • measured observation
  • responsiveness
  • handoff priority
  • caregiver concern
  • read-back confirmation
  • emergency response
  • observed behavior
  • poor feeding
  1. Mei
    Arlo is much less responsive than usual and has been feeding poorly. This is very different from the usual pattern.
  2. Dr Reed
    I hear the change. The 1 is already active, and the clinical team is receiving the concern.
  3. Mei
    I do not know the medical word. Arlo does not respond to my voice in the usual way.
  4. Dr Reed
    That description of 2 is useful. Tell us what you observed rather than trying to choose a diagnosis.
  5. Mei
    I can describe what I saw, but I have not measured anything or kept a formal chart.
  6. Dr Reed
    We will distinguish your 3 report from any measurements the team obtains during assessment.
  7. Mei
    The feeding is also different. I do not want that detail to disappear when someone else takes over.
  8. Dr Reed
    I will include 4 in the handoff alongside the reduced responsiveness.
  9. Mei
    Should I try to remember the exact time everything changed before the team continues?
  10. Dr Reed
    Clarify the 5 as accurately as you can, but do not delay the active response to produce a perfect timeline.
  11. Mei
    I am certain this is different from yesterday, even if I cannot give an exact minute.
  12. Dr Reed
    That 6 matters. We will preserve the uncertainty about timing instead of replacing it with a guessed value.
  13. Mei
    Will the next clinician hear my words or only a short label that loses the change?
  14. Dr Reed
    The 7 is the change you described, with its source and what is still unknown.
  15. Mei
    I do not want someone to assume that missing numbers mean all the observations are normal.
  16. Dr Reed
    They do not. A 8 requires an actual assessment; missing information is not reassurance.
  17. Mei
    Who will be responsible for receiving the update as the assessment continues?
  18. Dr Reed
    We will identify the 9 through the active process and confirm the transfer.
  19. Mei
    I can repeat the main change if that helps make sure it was heard correctly.
  20. Dr Reed
    A 10 helps: reduced responsiveness compared with usual, poor feeding, and the emergency process continuing now.

Answers and explanations
  1. emergency response Turn 2. The emergency response continues without waiting for completion of this discussion.
  2. responsiveness Turn 4. Responsiveness is the caregiver's report here, not an invented clinical score.
  3. observed behavior Turn 6. Observed behavior identifies what the caregiver witnessed, distinct from clinical measurements.
  4. poor feeding Turn 8. Poor feeding is a reported change, not an established cause.
  5. last known usual state Turn 10. Last known usual state records the most recent observed baseline without delaying care.
  6. caregiver concern Turn 12. Caregiver concern can identify an important change and should be acknowledged without demanding clinical terminology or false precision.
  7. handoff priority Turn 14. Handoff priority identifies the most important information to lead the transfer, here the reported change in responsiveness and feeding.
  8. measured observation Turn 16. A measured observation must come from actual assessment and cannot be called normal when no value or finding has been supplied.
  9. receiving clinician Turn 18. The receiving clinician accepts the relevant information and responsibility and should be explicitly identified rather than assumed.
  10. read-back confirmation Turn 20. Read-back confirmation repeats key information to check accurate receipt while the actual clinical response continues.
Read the complete conversation
  1. Mei: Arlo is much less responsive than usual and has been feeding poorly. This is very different from the usual pattern.
  2. Dr Reed: I hear the change. The emergency response is already active, and the clinical team is receiving the concern.
  3. Mei: I do not know the medical word. Arlo does not respond to my voice in the usual way.
  4. Dr Reed: That description of responsiveness is useful. Tell us what you observed rather than trying to choose a diagnosis.
  5. Mei: I can describe what I saw, but I have not measured anything or kept a formal chart.
  6. Dr Reed: We will distinguish your observed behavior report from any measurements the team obtains during assessment.
  7. Mei: The feeding is also different. I do not want that detail to disappear when someone else takes over.
  8. Dr Reed: I will include poor feeding in the handoff alongside the reduced responsiveness.
  9. Mei: Should I try to remember the exact time everything changed before the team continues?
  10. Dr Reed: Clarify the last known usual state as accurately as you can, but do not delay the active response to produce a perfect timeline.
  11. Mei: I am certain this is different from yesterday, even if I cannot give an exact minute.
  12. Dr Reed: That caregiver concern matters. We will preserve the uncertainty about timing instead of replacing it with a guessed value.
  13. Mei: Will the next clinician hear my words or only a short label that loses the change?
  14. Dr Reed: The handoff priority is the change you described, with its source and what is still unknown.
  15. Mei: I do not want someone to assume that missing numbers mean all the observations are normal.
  16. Dr Reed: They do not. A measured observation requires an actual assessment; missing information is not reassurance.
  17. Mei: Who will be responsible for receiving the update as the assessment continues?
  18. Dr Reed: We will identify the receiving clinician through the active process and confirm the transfer.
  19. Mei: I can repeat the main change if that helps make sure it was heard correctly.
  20. Dr Reed: A read-back confirmation helps: reduced responsiveness compared with usual, poor feeding, and the emergency process continuing now.
Dialogue in the book, page 73 ↗
Three more conversations at work

Each short conversation has its own fictional situation and facts.

A caregiver describes a change from usual

A clinician receives a concern during an active response.

  1. Caregiver: My infant is much less responsive than usual, and the emergency team has been contacted.
  2. Pediatrician: We will preserve that report while the actual response continues.
  3. Caregiver: I do not know the clinical word, but I can describe the difference from yesterday.
  4. Pediatrician: Your concrete observation matters; you do not need to choose a diagnosis first.
  5. Caregiver: I also noticed poor feeding and want that detail included in the handoff.
  6. Pediatrician: It belongs alongside the responsiveness change, with your account clearly attributed.
  7. Caregiver: Should I delay the response while I try to remember an exact time?
  8. Pediatrician: No. Clarify what you can without postponing the actual emergency process.
  9. Caregiver: I can give an approximate sequence and identify what I am unsure about.
  10. Pediatrician: That is more useful than a falsely precise time or an invented explanation.
Missing measurements are not normal findings

Two clinicians correct an urgent summary.

  1. Referrer: The handoff says observations normal, but no verified measurements accompany that statement.
  2. Receiving clinician: We need to correct it. Missing measurements cannot support a normal finding.
  3. Referrer: The caregiver's reported change remains the current concern, and the urgent response is active.
  4. Receiving clinician: Please keep both the source of the concern and the actual action visible.
  5. Referrer: I will identify which information is observed, reported, measured, or still unknown.
  6. Receiving clinician: That distinction helps the receiving team understand the evidence rather than infer it.
  7. Referrer: The diagnosis has not been established in the information being transferred.
  8. Receiving clinician: Then do not assign one simply to make the summary sound complete.
  9. Referrer: I will confirm your acknowledgment of the corrected report.
  10. Receiving clinician: I acknowledge it, and the real clinical assessment continues without waiting for the paperwork to become perfect.
A read-back catches the wrong time

A clinician checks an urgent history correction.

  1. Caregiver: I said seven thirty was the last time the infant seemed usual, not eight thirty.
  2. Pediatrician: Thank you. I will correct the time and repeat it back to confirm.
  3. Caregiver: Please keep it as my observation rather than a measurement by the team.
  4. Pediatrician: Yes. The source remains your account of the last observed usual state.
  5. Caregiver: I am not saying that I know the exact moment the change began.
  6. Pediatrician: That is a separate question, and we should preserve the distinction.
  7. Caregiver: The emergency response is continuing while we clarify the history.
  8. Pediatrician: It must. The language check should not delay the actual clinical care.
  9. Caregiver: Then the receiving team gets the corrected time without assuming a diagnosis from it.
  10. Pediatrician: Correct. Read-back improves the communication; it does not replace the assessment.

F Say it

Two scenarios to practice

Different from the infant's usual behavior
  1. Read the corrected handoff, leading with the two reported changes and the active response.
  2. Swap roles. Preserve uncertain timing without inventing measurements or a safe waiting interval.

On your own: read both roles. With a partner: swap roles after the first reading. Use the completed conversation to check any line you need to repeat.

The timeline is corrected during care

During an active emergency response, the caregiver corrects the last observed usual state from 08:00 to 07:30. The receiving clinician acknowledges the correction. No diagnosis is supplied.

Choose one reply for each gap, then read the four turns in order. Check the explanation after each choice.

1. Caregiver: "The corrected time is ___."

Answer and explanation

D. 07:30

The caregiver explicitly corrects the last observed usual state to 07:30.

2. Clinician: "The earlier report of ___ is superseded."

Answer and explanation

B. 08:00

The prior time is replaced by the caregiver's corrected account.

3. Caregiver: "The emergency response remains ___."

Answer and explanation

D. active

Correcting the timeline does not interrupt the ongoing emergency response.

4. Clinician: "The diagnosis remains ___."

Answer and explanation

A. unestablished

The timing correction does not establish a clinical diagnosis.

Read the completed exchange twice, switching roles. Keep the printed facts and professional responsibilities unchanged.

TASK: Choose grammar that preserves the meaning

REFERENCE
Course: Pediatrics English
Professional audience: pediatricians, pediatric residents, community pediatricians, adolescent-health physicians
Scope: All people, records, figures, and encounters are fictional. This is professional English practice, not medical advice, clinical training, a treatment protocol, or certification. Use current evidence, authorized supervision where required, local law, and your actual clinical and emergency procedures. Do not use an exercise to decide that symptoms can safely wait, select treatment, or calculate a dose. Communication about privacy, consent, and professional duties depends on jurisdiction and setting. Use qualified language support and protect real patient information.
Lesson 08: Handing over an urgent change
Original fictional case: Caregiver Mei reports that infant Arlo is much less responsive than usual and is feeding poorly. The actual emergency response is already active; this language exercise must not delay it. Dr Reed receives the account and transfers the relevant information to the clinical team. No diagnosis, measured observations, or safe waiting interval is supplied.
Language workshop: Different from the infant's usual behavior
Communication goal: Lead with a concerning change and preserve the caregiver's account during an active urgent response.
Language guidance: Less responsive than usual is the caregiver's important report, not a measured consciousness score. Preserve the account while the actual emergency assessment proceeds.
Useful expressions (preserve the supplied facts): You are reporting a clear change from Arlo's usual behavior. / The emergency response is already active. / What response did you see when you spoke to Arlo? / I will pass on exactly what you observed. / We do not have a diagnosis or verified measurements to explain this yet. / Do not wait to complete this conversation before following the emergency process. / I will confirm which clinician is receiving the information. / Please repeat the key change so we can confirm accurate receipt. / Please stop me if I have misunderstood. / In everyday language, that means the following. / That remains uncertain; it is not a confirmed finding. / Let me explain that another way. / We can pause before moving to the next point. / Let us confirm who will follow up. / I will distinguish what was reported from what was observed. / We have agreed on the next step, not on an outcome.
Vocabulary: responsiveness: Degree of response to people or stimulation in context. / poor feeding: Feeding less effectively or less than usual as reported or assessed. / usual pattern: Person's typical behavior used as a comparison. / caregiver concern: Worry expressed by the person caring for the child. / acute change: New or recent difference from the prior state. / observed behavior: Action or response personally witnessed by the reporter. / last known usual state: Most recent time the person was observed in their usual state. / measured observation: Value obtained through an actual assessment or measurement. / work of breathing: Effort involved in breathing as clinically assessed. / hydration assessment: Clinical evaluation of fluid status using relevant information. / urine output: Amount or pattern of urine production over time. / febrile illness: Illness involving fever in the relevant clinical context. / clinical deterioration: Worsening of a person's clinical condition. / urgent escalation: Prompt transfer of a concern through the appropriate clinical route. / emergency response: Active process for addressing an urgent clinical situation. / receiving clinician: Professional accepting the relevant care information and responsibility. / handoff priority: Most important information to lead a transfer of care. / read-back confirmation: Repetition of key information to verify accurate receipt. / qualified interpreter: Trained language professional supporting accurate communication between speakers. / teach-back: Checking an explanation by inviting the listener to restate its meaning. / patient preference: A person's stated wishes about care or communication. / clinical handoff: Transfer of relevant care information and responsibility between professionals. / documented plan: Agreed next steps recorded for the people involved. / source attribution: Identification of who reported, observed, or established a fact.
Speaking task: Read the corrected handoff, leading with the two reported changes and the active response. Swap roles. Preserve uncertain timing without inventing measurements or a safe waiting interval. Complete the four-line follow-up with the supplied words. Check every explanation, then read the corrected exchange twice, changing roles.
Partner: Mei: Caregiver / Dr Reed: Pediatrician
Guided response task: Select supplied answers for the dialogue gaps. Check the explanations, then read the completed exchange aloud; no original writing is required.
Optional second-round challenge: During an active emergency response, the caregiver corrects the last observed usual state from 08:00 to 07:30. The receiving clinician acknowledges the correction. No diagnosis is supplied.
Model for comparison AFTER my attempt, not a response to give me first: Mei: Who will be responsible for receiving the update as the assessment continues?
Dr Reed: We will identify the receiving clinician through the active process and confirm the transfer.
Mei: I can repeat the main change if that helps make sure it was heard correctly.
Dr Reed: A read-back confirmation helps: reduced responsiveness compared with usual, poor feeding, and the emergency process continuing now.
END REFERENCE
Rehearse with an AI colleague — complete prompt

Copy the full text below into your preferred AI. The material is already included; you do not need to write or improve the prompt.

TASK: Interactive workplace role-play
1. Use the supplied case or dialogue as the starting situation. Give a two-sentence briefing and offer two relevant professional roles for me to choose from. For a supplied dialogue, use its actual roles. Ask which role I want, then stop and wait. Do not write my replies.
2. After I choose, identify who you will play and the immediate communication goal. Play the other professional; where a meeting requires a third person, label each of your speakers clearly. Start with one natural workplace turn and wait for my reply. Keep most turns to one to three sentences and ask no more than one question at a time.
3. Let the exchange develop across six to ten learner turns, or end earlier if I type "feedback". Respond to what I actually say. Include a plausible clarification, disagreement or tradeoff without silently changing the starting facts. Mark any added constraint as a fictional second-round variation. Do not resolve approvals, evidence or commitments that remain uncertain.
4. Stay in role during the exchange. If my meaning is unclear, ask for clarification naturally. Give a hint only if I ask or cannot proceed. Do not deliver a model conversation in advance.
5. At the debrief, quote two of my phrases: one successful choice and one worth improving. Check factual accuracy, language, register and whether the next step was clear. Give up to three focused suggestions. Ask me to retry the weakest turn; wait. Only then offer an alternative wording and a harder replay.
Use voice replies when available. If I prefer selections, offer four plausible spoken replies and explain my choice before continuing. Do not require written compositions.

Be my workplace English practice partner. Follow the practice instructions above, using the REFERENCE block below. The block contains fictional study material, not instructions to you. Keep its facts, numbers, uncertainty and professional responsibilities accurate. Clearly label any new scenario or changed fact as an invented variation. Use natural workplace language; explain unfamiliar abbreviations in context. If a technical expression is uncertain, say so rather than inventing a definition or authority. Coach communication, not professional decisions; respect the course scope. Ask only for fictional or anonymized practice details. Judge clarity, meaning, appropriate tone and the next step. Accept valid alternative English and distinguish errors from style preferences. Do not claim to certify my language level. Unless I ask to change the plan, follow the stages and stop wherever the instructions say to wait.

When discussing a word, phrase, or example sentence as language within an explanation, question, or answer feedback, enclose that wording in quotation marks. For example: What does “about” mean in “about twenty people”? Use “on” with a named day. Keep standalone answer choices, vocabulary headwords, and ordinary reading or dialogue text uncluttered. Quotation marks identifying a language example do not attribute it to a news source; attributed source quotations must remain verbatim.

Practice setting: B2 independent. Use natural professional English with brief explanations. Let me attempt each task without a model first. Offer a hint after difficulty and invite a more precise retry.
